During the RTL design phase this is helpful to code the design.
Implement the RTL using different procedure blocks for the following

--- Page 124 ---
1. Edge or level detection logic.  
2. Priority detection logic.  
3. Interrupt status and branching/scheduling of the interrupt logic. 
5.5 RTL Top-Level Design and Synthesis [1]
The design for the processor can be efficiently achieved using the modular
design approach or the bottom-up approach. Have the design partitioning at
the architecture level and use the reference document as micro-architecture
and code the RTL for the
1. Top_RTL.vhd
a. ALU.vhd  
b. Internal_memory.vhd 
c. Bus_interface.vhd 
d. Internal_registers.vhd 
e. Interrupt_conttrol.vhd 
f. Timing_control.vhd
i. Timers_counter.vhd  
g. Serial_IO_control.vhd 
 
Use the synchronizers, tristate logic and clock reset network in the top
block.

--- Page 125 ---
5.5.1 Block-Level Design
The team members (RTL design, Verification, Synthesis, and STA team)
can perform the following:
1. Code the efficient RTL for each functional block.  
2. Verify the design using the required tests.  
3. Use the block-level constraints and synthesize the design. 
4. Check for whether the constraints can meet or not?  
5. Perform the timing simulation and pre-layout STA. Fix the setup time
violations using the RTL tweaks, architecture tweaks.  
5.5.2 Top-Level Design
The team can perform following tasks
1. Create the Top.vhd using the instantiation and verify the design. 
2. Check for the coverage goals met or not?  
3. Synthesize the design using top-level constraints.  
4. Check for the constraints met or not?  
5. Perform the pre-layout timing for the top.vhd.  
6. Check for the timing violations and fix them using the necessary
tweaks.  
5.6 Strategies for SOC Verification
To check the functional correctness of the design, the testbench can be used.
The driver or stimulus generator can force the required signals such as ‘clk,’
‘reset_n,’ and ‘data_in” according to the design requirements. But this is

--- Page 126 ---
useful for the design which has a moderate gate count. For the complex
SOCs, the verification needs to be carried out at block level, top level, and
chip level.
Most of the time is utilized to find the functional bugs in design, and to
save the time, the process can be automated using the HVLs and the self-
checking layered testbenches.
The objective is to fulfill the specified coverage goals. The HVLs such
as System Verilog are popular in the industries and used for coverage driven
verification.
The RTL verification for the complex design can consume almost
around 65–70% of the overall design cycle time, and the following can be
done to achieve the coverage goals.
1. Better Verification planning: Have verification plan for the block-
level verification, top-level verification, and full-chip verification. 
2. Verification cycle and kickoff: Kickoff verification phase
simultaneously with RTL design phase and use the functional model as
golden reference during verification.
 
3. Test cases: By understanding the design functionality at block- and
chip-level, document the required test cases and use test plan to achieve
the specified block-level and chip-level coverage goals.
 
4. Randomize test cases: Create the test cases and randomize them to
carry out the verification for the block-level design.  
5. Better testbench architecture: Develop the automated multilayer
testbench architecture using driver, monitor, and scoreboards.  
6. Define coverage goals: Define the coverage goals such as functional,
code, toggle, and constrained randomized coverage at the block level
and at the chip level.
 
The testbench should perform the following:
1. Generate required stimulus.  

--- Page 127 ---
2. Apply stimulus to the DUT or DUV.  
3. It should capture the response.  
4. It should check for the functional correctness of the design. 
5. It should be used to measure progress against the overall verification
goals.  
What need to be thought about the design inputs while randomizing?
1. Information about the device configuration.  
2. Information about the environment configuration.  
3. What is the input data stream and information about the input packets. 
4. What are the different protocols and what are the protocol exceptions? 
5. What kind of delays and delay routines?  
6. Where to report about the errors and violations?  
The layered testbench architecture is shown in Fig. 5.10.

--- Page 128 ---
Fig. 5.10Layered testbench [1]
Command Layer: The command layer has the driver which drives the
command to the DUT, and the monitor captures the transition of the signals
and groups them together in the form of the command. Consider bus write
or read command in AHB. The assertions also drive the DUT.
Functional Layer: Above the command layer, the functional layer in
which the agent or transactor drives the driver after receiving the high-level
transaction such as DMA read and write. Such type of transaction can be
broken into multiple commands to drive the driver.
Scoreboard and checker: To predict the result of the transactions, these
commands are sent to the scoreboard and the checker compares the
commands from monitor with the scoreboard.
If we consider the H.264 Encoder, then to test for the multiple frame
processing, frame size, type, these parameters can be configured by using
the constrained random values of these parameters. This is what we call it
as creating the scenario to verify the particular functionality.

--- Page 129 ---
5.7 Important Points to Conclude the Chapter
The following are the important points to summarize this chapter.
1. Have a better definition of the design specification using the
architecture and micro-architecture document.  
2. During the design try to have a better understanding of the parallelism,
priority logic and interface speed.  
3. During the RTL design, try to have better understanding of the
functional blocks and their dependability.  
4. During top-level integration, use the configuration, test, and debug
logic.  
5. Try to identify the functional and timing proven IPs available in the
market, before RTL design kickoff.  
6. If IPs are used in the design, then understand the timing and interfaces
and create the wrappers for the connectivity.  
7. Have a better verification plan and test case generation for the block-
level, top-level and chip-level designs.  
8. Define the coverage goals for the constrained random verification. 
9. Use the layered testbench and monitor the desired coverage. 
This chapter has given us understanding about the RTL design and
verification strategies for the complex designs. Even we have discussed
about the architecture and micro-architecture of the basic
processor design. The next chapter discusses about the FSM, FSM
synthesis, performance improvement techniques and the buses, data
transfer mechanisms, bus arbiters, DSP based system architecture and
their requirements in the complex SOCs. The synthesis strategies will be
discussed with few important design scenarios.

6. VHDL Design and RTL Tweaks
Vaibbhav Taraate1 
 
1 Rupee S T (Semiconductor Training @ Rs. 1), Pune, Maharashtra,
India
 
Vaibbhav Taraate
Email: vaibbhavt@gmail.com
“The design performance can be improved using better
architecture and RTL tweaks.”
Abstract
During the RTL design phase, how RTL design team transforms
functionality using efficient VHDL constructs to infer the intended logic is
very important aspects. Using synthesizable constructs and modular
approach, the RTL at block level can be partitioned into multiple functional
blocks. The RTL tweaks and architecture tweaks before synthesis to infer
the intended logic can yield the better synthesis result for the required
constraints. In such circumstances, the chapter is useful to understand the
FSM design, optimization, RTL tweaks, and other performance
improvement techniques. The chapter also discusses the bus arbiters, data
transfer schemes, and how to think about the DSP-based system design.
KeywordsState machines – Moore – Mealy – Asynchronous reset –
Synchronous reset – Sequence detector – FSM synthesis – FSM
optimization – Bus topology – Arbiter – DSP processor – DSP elements –
VHDL – RTL tweaks – Performance improvement

--- Page 132 ---
Most of the time due to inefficient RTL, the synthesis tool will not be able
to infer the intended logic. For example, if the RTL designer wishes to infer
the parallel logic and the RTL is coded using the ‘if-then-else,’ then the
synthesis tool will infer the priority logic. Or if multiple sequential blocks
need to be executed in parallel and the RTL design has single ‘process’
block, then the synthesis tool will not be able to infer the parallelism. In
such circumstances, the required optimization and RTL tweaks are
discussed in the following few sections.
6.1 Reset Strategies
Reset is used for initialization of the design. We can have asynchronous or
synchronous reset depending on the design requirements. It can be active
low or active high reset. To avoid the issues due to reset, we can have the
reset tree.
6.1.1 Asynchronous Reset
This type of reset is independent of the active clock edge and hence called
as asynchronous reset. It is used to initialize the design irrespective of clock
edge. The RTL using VHDL is described in Example 1 and uses the
asynchronous reset.
Example 1: RTL Using Asynchronous Reset
library IEEE;
use IEEE.STD_LOGIC_1164.ALL;
entity asynchronous_reset is
    Port ( data_in : in std_logic;
          clk : in std_logic;
           reset_n : in std_logic;
           data_out : out std_logic );
end asynchronous_reset;
--Functional description of asynchronous reset

--- Page 133 ---
architecture arch_async_reset of asynchronous_reset is
begin
process ( clk , reset_n)
begin
                If ( reset_n=‘0’)  then
                                data_out  <= ‘0’;
                                elsif (clk = ‘1’ and clk’event) then 
                                data_out  <= data_in;
                end if;
end process;
end arch_async_reset;
The synthesis tool infers the logic shown in Fig. 6.1 for the asynchronous
reset. As shown, the inferred logic does not have any element in the reset
path. So this type of logic has the lesser area as compared to synchronous
reset.
Fig. 6.1Synthesis outcome for the asynchronous reset
6.1.2 Synchronous Reset
Initialization of the sequential element due to the synchronous reset is
dependent on the active edge of the clock. As described in Example 2, the

--- Page 134 ---
output of sequential cell is assigned to zero using the synchronous reset
which is sampled on the rising edge of the clock.
Example 2: RTL for Synchronous Reset
library IEEE;
use IEEE.STD_LOGIC_1164.ALL;
entity synchronous_reset is
    Port ( data_in : in std_logic;
              clk : in std_logic;
           reset_n : in std_logic;
           data_out : out std_logic );
end synchronous_reset;
-- Functional description of register using synchronous reset 
architecture arch_sync_reset of synchronous_reset is
begin
 process ( clk)
 begin
               if (clk = ‘1’ and clk’event) then 
                         If ( reset_n = ‘0’) then
                           data_out  <= ‘0’;
                           else
                            data_out  <= data_in;
                          end if;
               end if;
  end process;  
end arch_sync_reset;

--- Page 135 ---
The synthesis outcome is shown in Fig. 6.2, and as shown, the inferred
logic has the additional combinational element in the reset path. This
increases the area.
Fig. 6.2Synthesis result for synchronous reset
6.2 State Machines
The state machine controllers are used to detect sequence as well as to
generate the sequence. The Moore and Mealy state machines can be
designed using modular approach and the objective during RTL design is to
have the glitch-free output. In such kind of the designs, the intended goal of
the RTL design engineer is to have optimization with the clean timing.
Depending on the need, the designer can use the binary, gray, or one-hot
encoding schemes to achieve the required results.
The random counters, sequence detectors, and DRAM refresh sequence
can be coded by using the state machine.
6.2.1 Moore Machine
In the Moore machine, the output is function of the current state only. It
needs to wait for one clock cycle to reflect the change at the output after
changes in the input. This type of machine needs one more extra state as
compared to Mealy machine.
The following are few highlights of Moore FSM:

--- Page 136 ---
1. In the Moore FSM, the output is function of current state only.  
2. As output is function of the current state, it is stable for one clock
cycle.  
3. Due to shorter combinational path, the STA is simple for the controllers
designed using the Moore FSM. This results in higher operating
frequency as compared to Mealy machine.
 
4. It needs more states as compared to the Mealy machine, but the output
is glitch-free as it is function of the current state only (Fig. 6.3).
Fig. 6.3Moore machine block diagram
 
6.2.2 Mealy Machine
In the Mealy machine, the output is function of the current state and input
also. This type of machine can result in the output with glitches. This type
of machine needs one less state as compared to Mealy machine if coded by
binary encoding techniques.
The following are few highlights of Mealy FSM:
1. In the Mealy FSM, the output is function of current state and input also. 
2. As output is function of the current state and input, it can change
multiple times during clock period.  

--- Page 137 ---
3. Due to larger combinational path, the STA is complex for the
controllers designed using the Mealy FSM. This results in the lower
operating frequency as compared to Moore machine.
 
4. It needs less states as compared to the Moore machine, but the output is
prone to glitches (Fig. 6.4).
Fig. 6.4Mealy machine block diagram
 
Few tricks to improve the Performance of the FSMs:
To improve the performance of the FSMs, use following techniques at the
RTL level.
1. Do not use single ‘process’ block to code the FSMs as it does not yield
into the efficient results.  
2. To achieve the efficient synthesis for the FPGA/ASIC, use the multiple
‘process’ block. In practice, we can think of using.
a. Use separate process block for the next state logic which should
infer the combinational logic.  
b. Use another process block for the state register and sensitive to the
active edge of the clock.  
c. Third process block for the output logic and it should infer the
combinational logic.  
 

--- Page 138 ---
3. Use level-sensitive process block for the next state and output logic as
they are combinational in nature.  
4. Use the clk = ‘1’ and clk’ event or clk='0' and clk'event inside the state
register block as this block is triggered on the active clock edge may be
positive or negative.
 
5. Use the desired encoding method.
a. Binary encoding needs n flip-flops for the 2n states  
b. Gray encoding needs n flip-flops for the 2n states  
c. One-hot encoding needs 2n flip-flops for the 2n states. 
 
6. To avoid the latch inference, use the ‘when others’ condition or cover
all the case conditions while using the case construct. 
7. To assign the current state value depending on the next state logic
output, use the ‘if-then-else’ construct and take care that the intended
logic should be sensitive to the active edge of clock.
 
8. If area is not a bottleneck in the design and clean timing is required,
then use one-hot encoding FSMs.  
9. For glitch-free output, use the registered output.  
6.3 RTL Design of Moore Machine
As discussed earlier in the Moore machine, the output is function of the
current state. To detect the sequence of ‘101,’ the RTL is described using
the synthesizable constructs and is shown in the Example 3. The synthesis
outcome for the Moore FSM is shown in Fig. 6.5.

--- Page 139 ---
Fig. 6.5Synthesis outcome of Moore machine to detect “101”
Example 3: RTL Design Using VHDL for Moore Machine
library ieee;
use ieee.std_logic_1164.all;
entity moore_machine is
Port ( clk : in std_logic;
data_in : in std_logic;
reset_in : in std_logic;
data_out : out std_logic);
end moore_machine;
architecture arch_moore of moore_machine is
type state_type is ( S0, S1, S2, S3);
signal present_state, next_state : state_type;
begin
              State_register : process (clk, reset_in)
              begin

--- Page 140 ---
                     if (reset_in  =  ‘0’) then
                   present_state  <= S0;
                   elsif (clk = ‘1’ and clk’event ) then
                   present_state  <= next_state;
                   end if;
end process;
next_state_logic : process(present_state, data_in)
begin
            next_state  <= S0; 
            case (present_state) is 
            when S0  =>if (data_in  =  ‘1’) then
            next_state  <= S1;
            else
            next_state  <= S0; 
            end if; 
        when S1  =>if (data_in  =  ‘1’) then
           next_state  <= S1;
           else
           next_state  <= S2;
           end if; 
        when S2  => if (data_in  =  ‘1’) then
           next_state  <= S3;
           else
           next_state  <= S0; 
           end if; 
        when S3  => if (data_in  =  ‘1’) then
           next_state  <= S1;
           else
           next_state  <= S2; 
           end if; 

--- Page 141 ---
           when others=> next_state  <=S0; 
           end case; 
    end process;
 output_comb_logic : process(present_state) 
  begin 
             case (present_state) is 
            when S0  => data_out  <= ‘0’; 
            when S1  => data_out  <= ‘0’; 
            when S2  => data_out  <= ‘0’; 
            when S3  => data_out  <= ‘1’; 
            when others  => data_out  <= ‘0’;
            end case;
  end process;
end arch_moore;
6.4 RTL Design of Mealy Machine
In the Mealy machine, the output is function of the current state and input .
To detect the sequence of ‘101,’ the RTL is described using the
synthesizable constructs and is shown in Example 4. The synthesis outcome
for the Mealy FSM is shown in Fig. 6.6.
Fig. 6.6Synthesis outcome of Mealy machine to detect “101”

--- Page 142 ---
Example 4: RTL Design Using VHDL for Mealy Machine
library ieee;
use ieee.std_logic_1164.all;
entity mealy_machine is
Port ( clk : in std_logic;
data_in : in std_logic;
reset_in : in std_logic;
data_out : out std_logic);
end mealy_machine;
architecture arch_mealy of mealy_machine is
type state_type is ( S0, S1, S2, S3);
signal present_state, next_state : state_type;
begin
             State_register : process (clk, reset_in)
             begin
                 if (reset_in  =  ‘0’) then
                 present_state  <= S0;
                 elsif (clk = ‘1’ and clk’event ) then
                 present_state  <= next_state;
                end if;

--- Page 143 ---
end process;
next_state_logic_and_output_decoder : process(present_state, data_in)
begin
data_out  <= ‘0’; 
case (present_state) is 
  when S0  =>  if (data_in  =  ‘1’) then
            next_state  <= S1;
            data_out  <= ‘0’;
            else
            next_state  <= S0;
            data_out  <= ‘0’; 
                        end if; 
  when S1  => if (data_in  =  ‘1’) then
          next_state  <= S1;
          data_out  <= ‘0’;
          else
          next_state  <= S2;
          data_out  <= ‘0’; 
          end if; 
  when S2  => if (data_in  =  ‘1’) then

--- Page 144 ---
            next_state  <= S1;
            data_out  <= ‘1’;
            else
            next_state  <= S0;
            data_out  <= ‘0’; 
           end if; 
  when others  => next_state  <= S0;
                data_out  <= ‘0’;
  end case;
 end process;
end arch_mealy;
6.5 FSM Synthesis and Optimization
The FSM described using VHDL can be synthesized to get the clean timing
and lesser area. In such scenario, the better partitioning and the optimization
techniques can be used. The FSM synthesis is elaborated in Chaps. 8 and 9
with more scenarios.
Have separate design for FSM controller: Use the design partitioning
at functional or synthesis level to have dedicated logic for the FSM
controllers. After synthesis, if the performance is met then use
‘don’t_touch’ attribute to avoid the resynthesis.
Isolate the state machine controllers: If the SOC design has multiple
clock domain and if they need the FSM controllers, then isolate the FSM
controllers. Use the sequential boundaries and group the logic depending
on the clock regions.

--- Page 145 ---
Clean timing and optimization: For the better timing, use the one-hot
encoding FSM, but this will effect on the area.
Modular design approach: If the design requirement is of the multiple
FSMs, then code the separate RTL for each FSM controller.
Use of the FSM compiler: For the better design partitioning of the logic
and to extract the states in the form of state table, use the FSM compiler.
Optimization for the glitch-free output: Code the FSM with glitch-free
output and it can be ensured by having the sequential output boundaries.
Shorter combinational delay: Overall, timing performance of FSM can
be improved by reducing the combinational delay of the next state logic.
This will result in the better FSM performance.
The FSM controller synthesis result should infer the separate logic for the
‘next state,’ ‘state register,’ and ‘output.’
6.6 Data Transfer and Arbiter Design
To transfer the data between the different functional blocks, commonly high
speed buses are used. But this can be also accomplished by use of the
shared memory, registers with read/write capabilities, FIFOs, and even
using the bus protocols.
Most of the time, during the design we use the buses that can be high-
speed buses on which processor can initiate transactions or low-speed buses
which can be used for the slower data transfer. So during the architecture
evolution, the strategies need to be defined to model the buses. Instead of
using the single bus, it is advisable to use the multiple buses depending on
the data rate required.
Commonly used buses for serial data transfer are I2C, SPI and even the
asynchronous data transfer can be accomplished by controlling baud rate of
UART.
For the high-end SOC designs, the speed of the design depends upon the
buses used for communication. The AHB/APB buses can be good choice
for large density SOC designs. The IPs of these type of buses are available
in the market, and as they are functional and timing proven, they can be
used during SOC design phase.
As multiple design blocks are using the common buses, the RTL design
team should be able to understand the required bus arbitration schemes, and
these can be deployed during design phase. Few of the arbitration schemes

--- Page 146 ---
are daisy chaining, round-robin, static arbitration, etc. The following section
discusses the bus arbiter.
6.6.1 Bus Arbitration
The shared bus can be shared by multiple functional modules (components)
in the design environment. Depending on the request generated by one of
the modules, the bus can be granted if it is not busy.
The bus arbitration is used to sample the requests generated by the
different SOC components coupled with shared bus and to grant the request
to one of the SOC components.
As shown in Fig. 6.7, the multiple components (module 1 to module n)
generate the request to the bus arbiter and waits for the grant signals from
the arbiters. After receiving the grant signal from the bus arbiter, one of the
components gains the control of the shared bus.
Fig. 6.7Bus arbitrations [1]
In the practical environment, there are many schemes to design the bus
arbitration, and they can be daisy chaining, round-robin, and static
arbitrations. Depending on the design requirements, these schemes can be
used in the SOC designs (Fig. 6.8).

--- Page 147 ---
Fig. 6.8Synthesis outcome of static arbiter scheme
The static arbitration using VHDL is described in Example 5. The
‘request_0’ has highest priority and ‘request_2’ has lowest priority. The
synthesis result is shown in the Fig. 6.8.
Example 5: RTL Design Using VHDL for Static Arbiter
Library ieee;
Use ieee.std_logic_1164.all;
Entity static_arbiter is
Port  (
 clk , reset_n, request_0, request_1, request_2 : in std_logic;
grant_0,grant_1,grant_2: out std_logic);
end static_arbiter;
architecture arch_arbiter of static_arbiter is
begin

--- Page 148 ---
         process (clk,reset_n,request_0, request_1,request_2)
         begin
         if (reset_n = ‘0’) then
                 grant_0 <=‘0’;
                 grant_1 <=‘0’;
                 grant_2 <=‘0’;
         elsif(clk = ‘1’ and clk’event) then 
                 grant_0 <= request_0;
                 grant_1  <= (request_1 and  (not request_0));
                 grant_2 <=( request_2 and (not(request_1 or request_0)));
         end if;
         end process;
end arch_arbiter;
Now, let us consider how we can add the bus grant signals for the multiple
bus requests. Consider the bus is shared by the processor, high-speed data
controller, and memory controller. As it is shared bus, if bus is not busy
then the grant signal needs to be provided to the functional block which has
requested for bus. In such scenario, the bus arbitration needs to be deployed
to avoid bus contention and to accomplish the data transfer between the
elements.
The RTL for the arbiter with bus busy input is described in Example 6.
The synthesis outcome is shown in Fig. 6.9.

--- Page 149 ---
Fig. 6.9Synthesis outcome of bus arbiter with busy signal
Example 6: RTL Design Using VHDL for the Arbiter with Busy
Signal
library ieee;
use ieee.std_logic_1164.all;
Entity arbiter1 is
Port  (
 clk , reset_n, request_0, request_1, request_2 : in std_logic;
busy_bus : in std_logic;
grant: out std_logic_vector (2 downto 0));
end arbiter1;
architecture arch_arbiter of arbiter1 is
 signal busy_bus_1, busy_bus_2 :  Std_logic;
begin
         p1: process ( clk, reset_n)

--- Page 150 ---
    begin
         if (reset_n = ‘0’) then 
         busy_bus_1  <=‘0’;      
         elsif(clk = ‘1’ and clk’event) then
      busy_bus_1 <= busy_bus;
         end if;
    endprocess p1; 
         p2: process (busy_bus, busy_bus_1 )
    begin
    if (busy_bus = ‘0’ and busy_bus_1 = ‘1’ ) then  
    busy_bus_2  <=‘1’;
    else
    busy_bus_2  <=‘0’; 
    end if;
    end process p2;
         p3: process (clk,reset_n) 
         begin
         if (reset_n = ‘0’) then
         grant  <=“000”;
         elsif ( clk = ‘1’ and clk’event) then 
         if (busy_bus_2 = ‘1’) then 
         grant  <=“000”;
         elsif (busy_bus = ‘0’) then 
         grant(0)  <= request_0;
         grant(1)  <= request_1 and not(request_0);
         grant(2)  <= request_2 and not (request_1) and not(request_0);
         end if;

--- Page 151 ---
         end if;
         end process;
         end arch_arbiter;
6.7 DSP-Based Designs and RTL Strategies
While architecting for the SOC for the DSP applications, what we need to
think? This is answered in this section
1. What kind of DSP processor core I can choose?
DSP applications require high bandwidth due to complexity of high-
end data processing algorithms. So most of the time, we will try to use
available IPs during SOC design phase. The capability of IPs and core
decides overall speed, throughput for the design.
a. The core should have capability to perform the floating point
operations.  
b. For DSP applications, larger amount of internal memory and
storage registers are required. So the core should have the
capability to have the required registers which can be used during
the computation.
 
c. The additional need is the circular buffers and FIFO mechanisms to
support the queuing of the data.  
d. The core should have the high-density multipliers and large density
accumulators and shifters. These can be used during filtering
operations.
 
e. The architecture should support the separate program and data
memory access as well as pipelining and multitasking features. 
f. The core should have additional data transfer interfaces for high-
speed data transfer.  
 

--- Page 152 ---
2. The core should have on-chip DMA capability: For the burst of the
data transfer, the DSP system should have the on-chip DMA controller.
This will be used during the burst of data transfers without involving
the DSP processor. While designing the DSP systems, the speed of the
DMA is important parameter, and during the top level design, care
should be taken by the integration team to deploy the required
synchronizers for the multiple clock domain designs.
 
3. The core should have the serial interfaces: The capabilities like serial
data transfer using I2C or SPI can be added advantage. They can be
used to interface the eternal serial devices with the SOC [1]. Most of
the time, the serial devices are used to transfer or accept the serial data,
and in this scenario, the serial bus controller capabilities can be used.
 
4. The core should have the on-chip PLL: The phase locked loops are
used to generate the clock and the additional clock distribution logic for
the clock distribution with uniform clock skew [1]. So if we imagine
the clocking structure for the prototype using multiple FPGAs, then we
can imagine the clock tree which can generate clocks with uniform
clock skew.
 
5. The core should have the real-time data processing: Consider the
application as HD size video frame encoding, and for such applications,
the real-time data should be compressed and need to be outputted by
the encoder. In such scenarios, the real-time data, the timers, and real-
time clocking capabilities should be present in the DSP SOC [1].
 
6. Additional USB controllers for serial data transfer: The USB
interface for the serial data transfer between the host system and the
DSP processor core can be used in most of the architectures.
 
7. Additional analog blocks and interfaces: The analog blocks like
ADCs and DACs should be present for the data conversion. 
Additional with all above, the bus controllers and their capabilities for
high-speed data transfer need to be thought. By considering all these
features, the architecture of the SOC for the DSP capabilities can be

--- Page 153 ---
designed and evolved. Figure 6.10 describes important components of DSP
system.
Fig. 6.10Architecture for the DSP processing system [1]
6.8 Recommended RTL Tweaks
As discussed in previous few chapters, during the RTL design phase the
complex designs are partitioned across the sequential boundaries by
considering the functionality of the design. The Following can be few of the
important recommendations for RTL tweaks.

--- Page 154 ---
1. The RTL design for any functional block should result into the
intended logic. Now imagine the functionality is coded using the
single ‘case-end case’ constructs and has more than 16 conditions,
then it is recommended to tweak the RTL using multiple ‘case.’ This
will result in the improved parallelism and clean timing.
 
2. Now imagine another scenario, the design uses ‘if-then-else’ construct
and requirement is of parallel logic without any priority. In such
circumstances, the RTL can be tweaked using the multiple process
blocks and ‘case’ construct.
 
3. If the RTL uses the tristate logic, then tweak the logic using the
multiplexers. Although it is recommended to have tristate logic at top
level.
 
4. Consider the multiple clock domain design RTL, and then deploy the
synchronizers for ASIC design. For the FPGA designs, have the
separate synchronizer design (either level, pulse) and deploy them in
the required control paths.
 
5. Tweak the RTL using the grouping technique for getting the inference
of logic into two multiple data paths.  
6. Tweak the RTL by pushing the common resources at the output side
and chain of selection logic at the input side.  
7. If the multiple unused states are there in FSM controller, then tweak
the RTL to remove the redundant states or use the ‘when others’ to
initialize unused states output to the initialization sequence.
 
8. If the common factors or expressions are used during the RTL, then
tweak the RTL to avoid the repeated logic inference due to the
common factors.
 
9. Tweak the RTL in such a way that the design should have better
structure, readability, and better partitioning across the sequential
boundaries.
 

--- Page 155 ---
10.If FIFO RTL is developed and the issue is of the write and read
address generation, then tweak the RTL by using the Gray pointers. 

--- Page 156 ---
6.9 Important Points to Conclude the Chapter
The following are important points to conclude the chapter:
1. The FSM controller can be designed using the Moore or Mealy
machines.  
2. In the Moore machine, output is function of current state whereas in
Mealy machine output is function of the present input and current state. 
3. Use the multiple ‘process’ blocks during RTL design of state machines,
sequence generator.  
4. For clean timing, use the one-hot encoding FSM.  
5. For glitch-free output in Moore FSM, use the registered output and
monitor the behavior of the register to register path.  
6. Bus arbiters are used to avoid bus contention, and there are many
schemes like static arbiter, round-robin arbiter.  
7. Instead of using the single bus in SOC, try to use multiple buses for the
processor and peripheral.  
8. The DSP capabilities should include the floating point operations and
on-chip DMA controllers.  
This chapter has given us understanding about the FSM synthesis,
Moore, Mealy machine, and how to improve performance of the state
machine. Even we have discussed about the RTL tweaks, arbiter, and
how to explore the architecture for DSP-based applications. The next
chapter will discuss about the ASIC synthesis using Synopsys DC and
important DC commands used during synthesis.

“ASIC synthesis team should have better understanding about the
block level, top level and chip level design constraints.”
Abstract
The chapter discusses the ASIC synthesis and frequently used Synopsys DC
commands and their role during ASIC synthesis. Even the chapter discusses
about the design partitioning, synthesis guidelines, and design constraints.
The DC commands used during the synthesis and their role are described
for better understanding of synthesis scripts or to create synthesis scripts for
the required constratints. The chapter is useful to understand the area, speed
constraints, and logic synthesis using Synopsys DC.
KeywordsASIC – SOC – Logic synthesis – Physical synthesis –
Constraints – Area – Speed – Power – DC commands – Clock – Latency –
Generated clocks – False path – Multicycle path – Input delay – Output
delay – Timing report – Optimization – Clock network
7.1 Design Partitioning for Complex Designs
Most of the time, we discuss about the top level architecture with the better
partitioning of the design. The design partitioning for any ASIC or SOC is

--- Page 159 ---
important milestone to realize the efficient prototype with clean timing
path. SOC or ASIC design has analog and digital blocks, and design team
needs to have better understanding of the EDA tool chain used during
various milestones! During logic and physical syntheses, the objective of
team is to have the optimization of the design for the area, speed, and
power. The design can be partitioned at the architecture level, at synthesis
or netlist level. Under such circumstances, following can be thought process
while partitioning the complex designs.
1. Hardware and software partitioning: For the complex SOCs, the
software and hardware partitioning is the main requirement. The reason
being, the time instance at which software functional calls are executed
and the latency required to transfer or fetch the data plays important
role to get desired throughput. The main goal of architect team is to
have the low latency during high-speed data transfer. Which
functional blocks need to be implemented using the software and which
blocks need to be designed using Hardware Construction Language
(HCL) or HDL should be decided at the architecture level to achieve
better performance, speed, power, and bandwidth.
 
2. Partitioning for power domain isolation: During this decade, the real
requirement is low power and multiple power domain designs. The
power requirements for analog and digital blocks vary depending on
the logic density and so the fundamental question which we need to
address is, can we have strategy to isolate power domains depending on
the requirement of analog and digital blocks? The answer is ‘yes’ as
design should have analog and digital power domain isolations, and it
is recommended to have the low power architecture with the power
sequence.
 
3. Partitioning the design at sequential boundaries: Most of the time
for the clean timing the main requirement is efficient design
partitioning and that should be achieved at sequential boundaries. For
example, if we consider the H.264 encoder which needs the
functional blocks as prediction, quantization, transform, and entropy
coding, then we can partition the design at block level across sequential
boundaries. Again by using the modular approach, we can partition the
 

--- Page 160 ---
functional block-level design by using registered inputs and registered
outputs.
4. Partitioning by considering multiple clock domains: Most of the
SOCs have the multiple clocks. For example, consider the SOC which
has multiple processors, bus controller, and memory controller then in
such kinds of designs the processor, bus controller, and memory
controller may work at the different clock frequency. The immediate
need is to have the multiple clock domain architectures for such kind of
design. The strategies to deploy synchronizers need to be decided at top
level. So partitioning of the design by considering the processor as
CLKI domain, bus controller into CLKII domain, and memory
controller into CLKIII domain will be useful to create the seperate
design and path groups.
 
5. Partitioning for the FPGA-based Designs: FPGAs are good
candidates for the digital logic implementation. The SOC has digital
and analog blocks so partition the design to have separate prototype for
digital and analog blocks. Implementation strategy should be by using
the single or multiple FPGAs to implement the digital design. For the
analog blocks, such as ADCs, DACs, clocking structures, power
supply, and temperature monitoring/cooling blocks, use the separate
analog equivalent boards with required interfaces.
Few of the important highlights for such kind of design
implementation and prototype using FPGA are listed below.
a. Check whether the design needs single or multiple FPGA prototype 
b. Check for the IO availability and data transfer rate and partitioning
at sequential boundaries.  
c. If multiple FPGAs are required then use the required bus
connectivity/topology as daisy chaining, star or mixed
interconnect.
 
 
6. Partition to have modular FSM controllers: Most of the SOCs need
the power sequencing algorithms, DDR needs the refresh cycles, and
bus controller needs the high-speed data transfer. In such kind of
 

--- Page 161 ---
designs, use the modular approach while implementing the FSM
controllers. The target should be the clean timing and lesser area. Use
the gray or one-hot encoding techniques while implementing the FSM
controllers. Try to have the FSM controller design with glitch-free
output.
7. Partition for better configuration management: Partition the design
at the architecture level to have the dedicated configuration registers,
memories to initialize the sequence for the SOC.
 
7.2 ASIC Synthesis
As most of us are familiar with the term synthesis, the synthesis is process
of converting the design from the higher level of abstraction to the lower
level of abstraction. The logic and physical synthesis need to be carried out
to achieve the desired goals to have the minimum area and power. Another
important goal is that the design should meet the required speed constraints.
During the synthesis phase, the design team can use the optimization and
performance improvement techniques to achieve the better results. The
synthesis tool uses following inputs to perform the synthesis:
1. RTL design  
2. The ASIC libraries 
3. The design constraints. 
The result of the ASIC as well as FPGA synthesis is the gate-level
netlist. For ASIC designs, the ASIC synthesis tool uses the standard cells,
macros, DW components to have the gate-level netlist. During the FPGA
synthesis, the synthesis tool uses the FPGA resources such as CLBs, IOBs,
DSP blocks, BRAMs, distributed RAM, and hard processor IPs.
Consider the H.264 implementation using ASIC (Fig. 7.1). In such kind of
complex designs the startegy for the FPGA protoype and for the ASIC
design should be decided. The ASIC synthesis and FPGA synthesis will
differ in many ways and will be discussed in few subsequent chapters. 

--- Page 162 ---
Fig. 7.1Top level design of H.264 encoder
The Synopsys Design Compiler (DC) reads the standard cell libraries
and the RTL description to generate the technology-dependent gate-level
netlist. Synthesis is the process of converting the design from the higher
level of abstraction to the lower level of the design abstraction.
The synthesis tool should be efficient enough to optimize the design for
the given constraints. The inputs to the synthesis tool are libraries, RTL
description using, either VHDL or Verilog, and design constraints. The
output from the synthesis tool is gate-level netlist (Fig. 7.2).
Fig. 7.2Synthesis tool inputs and output
The synthesis tool performs many steps during the optimization to
generate the required gate-level netlist, and these steps are described in this
section:
DC reads the DesignWare, technology and symbol libraries to perform
the synthesis. The Synthesis tool is intelligent enough to identify the
components from the DesignWare and technology library. The technology

--- Page 163 ---
library consists of the logic gates, flip-flops, and latches. The complex
components like comparators, adders, multipliers are part of the
DesignWare library. By using these components efficiently either from the
DesignWare or technology library the DC performs the synthesis to
generate the gate-level netlist.
In the next step, the DC reads the RTL either written in the VHDL or
Verilog and proceeds to the next step where it can map to the link library if
it is in the gate-level form. The synthesis tool performs the optimization of
the RTL before mapping the cells to the technology or target library.
The synthesis tool uses the design constraints such as area, speed,
power, and the environmental constraints while performing synthesis. The
constraints are design specific and need to be specified for the desired or
required outcome of the design.
The main important point is that the RTL design and the synthesis team
should know about the target standard cell library so that the design can be
optimized to get the required functional outcome.
In the practical environment, while using the Synopsys DC always care
should be taken about required constraints those are mainly the area, speed,
and power. There are many designs in which the restriction is on the overall
area utilization. For the million gate design, synthesis and optimization is
iterative process as it requires the efficient design partitioning and even the
tight constraints to get the desired performance. For complex ASIC designs,
the RTL design, synthesis, and STA teams work closely to realize the
intended design functionality for the given constraints.
Following are the important steps during synthesis using Synopsys DC:
1. Read standard cell libraries  
2. Read Netlist  
3. Map to link library  
4. Apply design constraints  
5. Map and optimize for the target library 

--- Page 164 ---
6. Write optimized netlist.  
7.3 Synthesis Using Design Compiler (DC)
Synopsys Design Compiler (DC) is powerful synthesis tool used by leading
industries to perform the logic synthesis. This section discusses the
important DC commands used during logic synthesis
1. DC setup and environment: 
After invoking the Synopsys Design Compiler, it reads the start-up file
from the current working directory. The start-up file is synopsys_dc.setup.
There should be two start-up files: one should be in the current working
directory, and another should be in the root directory where the Design
Compiler is installed. To use the tool, the following important parameters
need to be setup.
1. search_path: This parameter is used to search for the synthesis
technology library as reference during synthesis.  
2. target_library: This parameter is used by the DC while mapping the
logic cells during synthesis. The target library consists of the logic
cells.
 
3. symbol_library: All the logic cells have symbolical representation
used for the visualization after synthesis. This parameter is used to pint
the library that contains the visual information for the logic cells
present in the technology synthesis library.
 
4. link_library: The tool uses the cells from the target_library for
mapping the desired functionality, this parameter is used to pint to the
reference of the logic gates in the synthesis technology library.
 
The above four parameters for .synopsys_dc.setup are described by
using following:
set search_path “./synopsys/libraries/syn/cell_library/syn”

--- Page 165 ---
set target_library “tcbn65lpwc.db, tcbn65lpbc.db”
set link_library “$target_library $symbol_library”
set symbol_library “standard.sldb dw_foundation.sldb”
Once the above variable or parameters are set up for the target library
then the synthesis tool can be invoked by using the command prompt.
2. Design Objects: The design objects are described in the following
table and used during synthesis. Every design is the description of the
logic circuit to perform some of the logical operations. The design can
be single system description or can consist of the multiple sub-systems.
The design objects are described in the Table 7.1.
Table 7.1Design objects used by synthesis tool
Design
object
Description
Cell Cell is also called as instance. The instantiated name of the sub-design is called
as cell
ReferenceIt is original design to which cell or instance refers. For example, instantiated
sub-design must refer to the design which consists of the functional description
of the sub-design
Ports The primary inputs and outputs or IO’s of the design are called as ports
Pins The primary inputs, outputs, and IO’s of cells in the design are called as pins
Net Wires used for the connection between ports of the pins of the different designs
are called as net
Clock The input port or pin used as clock source is called as clock
Library The technology-specific cells used for targeting for synthesis, linking or for
reference are called as library

1. The analyze and elaborate commands are used in order to pass required
parameters while elaborating the design.  
2. Using analyze and elaborate commands, the different architectures can
be specified during elaboration for the same analyzed design. 
3. The read command is used while entering for the pre-compiled designs
or netlists in DC.  
4. The read command does not allow the use of the different architectures.

7.4.2.1 DC Command to Define Clock
The clock is defined using the create_clock command. Table 7.5 describes
the clock definition commands.
Table 7.5Commands for clock definition
Command Description
create_clock –name
<clock_name>-period 
<clock_period>
<clock_pin_name>
Used to create the clock for the design and used as reference during
timing analysis. The clock is always associated with the clock pin of
the design. If design does not have clock then it will be treated as
virtual clock. For example, the following command can be used to
generate 500 MHz clock with 50% duty cycle is
create_clock –name clock -period 2 master_clock
If designer wishes to use the clock for variable duty cycle with rising
edge at 1 ns and clock period of 5 ns, then the same command can be
modified as
create_clock –name clock - period 5 –waveform {1,5} –name
master_clock
If the design does not have the clock pin, then the virtual clock is created
using the following commands.
This command generates virtual clock of frequency 200 MHz with 50%
duty cycle.
create_clock –name clock -period 5
This command generates virtual clock of frequency 200 MHz. with
variable duty cycle with rising edge at 1 ns and falling edge at 5 ns.
create_clock –name clock -period 5 –waveform {1,5}
7.4.3 DC Commands to Define Skew
The skew is difference between arrivals of the clock edges at various pins
of the flip-flop.
Positive Clock Skew: If clock at the destination flip-flop is delayed with
reference to the source flip-flop, then the skew is called as positive clock
skew and useful for the setup. The reason being, as clock at destination flip-
flop is delayed and due to that it allows that much additional time margin
for the design.

--- Page 169 ---
Negative Clock Skew: If clock at the source flip-flop is delayed with
reference to the destination flip-flop, then the skew is called as negative
clock skew and useful for the hold.
The Design Compiler will not be able to synthesize the clock tree so to
overcome the problem the clock skew is used to model the propagation
delay that exists in the clock tree.
Table 7.6 describes the commands used by DC while defining clock
skew for the design.
Table 7.6Commands used for the skew definitions
Command Description
set_clock_skew –rise_delay <rising_clock_skew>-
fall_delay <falling_clock_skew> <clock_name>
This command is used to define the clock
skew for the design. This can be described as
set_clock_skew –rise_delay 2 – fall_delay
1 master_clock

7.5 Design Optimization Constraints and Basic
Script
The design constraints such as area speed and power need to be met, and
this section discusses about few of the Synopsys DC commands used while
constraining the ASIC designs.
Following steps are performed by synthesis tool:
1. Synthesis tool reads the DesignWare libraries, technology, and symbol
libraries.  
2. Second step is to read the RTL described by Verilog.  
3. Perform the optimization to map the logic using the technology library
that is also called as target library.  
4. Use the design constraints like area, speed, and power and perform the
optimization.  
5. Map the design to target library and optimize the design.  
6. Finally, write the optimized netlist in the (.vhd) or (.ddc) format. 
The sample design constraint script is given below.
Example 1: Basic clk.src Script for 1 GHz Design
/*set the clock*/
set clock clk
/* set clock period */
set clock_period 1

--- Page 172 ---
/* set the latency */
set latency 0.025
/* set clock skew*/
set early_clock_skew [expr $ clock_period/10.0]
set late_clock_skew [expr $ clock_period/20.0]
/* set clock transition */
set clok_transition [expr $ clock_period/100.0]
/* set the external delay*/
set external_delay [expr $ clock-period*0.2]
/* define clock uncertainty */
set clock_uncertainty –setup $ early_clock_skew
set clock_uncertainty –hold $ late_clock_skew
Use the above script as clk.src and to generate the reports use the following
commands.
Example 2: Report Generation Commands
Dc_shell> report_timing
Dc_shell> report_clock
Dc_shell> report_constraints-all_violators
The timing report for the setup check is shown in Report 1. As shown in the
report, the set up slack which is difference between the data required time
and data arrival time is positive. This slack is met, and hence design does
not have any timing violation.
Report 1: Timing report for the setup analysis

--- Page 173 ---

clock source and clock network latency. Figure 7.3 shows both the
latencies.

--- Page 174 ---

Fig. 7.4Generated clock

7.8 Clock Muxing and False Paths

--- Page 175 ---
Most of the times, we need to have clock multiplexing. The minimum and
maximum frequency clocks can be used in the design depending on the
design requirements. During the ASIC testing, the minimum
frequency clock can be used. The false paths between these clocks need to
be reported to the timing analyzer. To set the false path, use the command
shown in Table 7.12 (Fig. 7.5).

The multicycle paths in the design need to be reported. The paths can be set

Fig. 7.7Multicycle path

Table 7.14Multicycle path commands

[get_ports clk_master]
To create the master clock of period 5 nsec

UFFO/Q] –to [get_pins UFF1/D]
This set the multicycle path of 3 cycles

UFFO/Q] –to [get_pins UFF1/D]
This is used to move the hold check to second
clock cycles as setup is checked at third clock

7.11 Important Points to Conclude the Chapter
The following are important points to conclude the chapter:
1. Synthesis is process of translating design from higher level of
abstractions to lower level of abstractions.  
2. Synopsys DC is powerful synthesis tool and used for logic synthesis. 
3. The synthesis tool uses the libraries, RTL design, and constraints
during the synthesis.  
4. The outcome of synthesis is gate-level netlist and used during pre-
layout STA.  
5. The synthesis team members should work on the block-level synthesis
and top-level synthesis.  
6. DC gives highest priority to optimize for the speed as compare to
the area.  
7. DC performs the design optimization to meet the constraints during
synthesis.  
8. If design constraints are not met, then the performance improvement
techniques at synthesis, RTL, and architecture level should be used. 
9. During synthesis phase, report the false paths and multicycle paths. 
This chapter has given us basic understanding about the frequently used
DC commands and how to use them. The next chapter discusses about
the design optimization using Synopsys DC and the details about how to
achieve the desired goal during synthesis.

“Synopsys DC is the efficient EDA tool and can be used for the

Abstract
The chapter discusses the design rule constraints and optimization
constraints used during the ASIC synthesis. The goal of this chapter is to
have understanding of the important DC commands and how they play the
important role during synthesis. The chapter also discusses about the
important design scenarios which may encounter during the ASIC
synthesis.
KeywordsASIC – DRC – Optimization – Fanout – Capacitance –
Transition – Max – Min – STA – Timing paths – Multicycle paths –

8.1 ASIC Synthesis and Design Constraints
During the design cycle which kicks off from the market survey to the
layout, we experience many challenges in each phase. The complex SOC
designs for the required process node need to meet the design constraints.

--- Page 180 ---
These constraints are mainly “Design Rule Constraints (DRC)” and
“Optimization Constraints”. So, it is very much required for the synthesis
team to understand the constraints at block, top and chip level. Depending
on the requirement of the speed, area, and power, the block-level and top-
level constraints can be extracted. Our objective in this chapter is to have
understanding of the optimization constraints and DRC.
To have better clarity about the synthesis, constraints, and optimization,
let us consider the top-level design which has complexity of almost around
200,000 logic cells.
As shown in Fig. 8.1 the multiple functional blocks are integrated at top
level. These blocks may be partitioned as
Fig. 8.1Top-level design of SOC

--- Page 181 ---
(1)Processing logic: The partition 1 which is  grouping of
functional blocks mainly processor, DSP logic, and Internal memory.  
(2)Controller and memory logic: The partition II can be the memory
controller and high-speed interfaces  
(3)IO and other interfaces: The third partition can be parallel and serial
IO  
(4)Test and Control logic: The fourth partition can be the test and
control logic for the SOC.  
By considering this, the block-level and top-level constraints can be
extracted depending on the speed, power, and area requirement for the
target process node.
Important steps to compile the design are shown in the Script 1
Script 1: Script to Compile the Design
/* read the design object */
read -format vhdl soc_top.vhd
/* specify the technology requirements */
target_library = my_library.db
symbol_library = my_library.sdb
link_library = ”*” + target_library
/* define the design environment */
set_load < value >< input_port>
.
.
.
set_load < value >< input_port>
set_driving_cell -cell FD1 all_inputs()
set_drive 0 clk_name
/* set the design constraints */
set_input_delay < value >-clock clk {< list of inputs >}
.
.
.

--- Page 182 ---

set_max_area 0
/* Perform the design synthesis*/
compile
/* generates reports */
report_constraint
report_area
/* save the design database */
write -format db -hierarchy -output soc_top.db

8.1.1 Block-Level Synthesis and Constraints
The block-level constraints to meet the desired area, speed, and the power
are specified using the tcl’tk commands.
Why it is essential to specify the block-level constraints is one of the
common questions? The few important points are listed below:
1. The complex SOC or ASIC designs can have the multiple functional
blocks and may have multiple clock domains, power domains, and the
functionality.
 
2. For the better optimization; the functionality of the design is partitioned
into the multiple domains to achieve the speed, area, and power
requirements, the design and optimization constraints need to be
specified.
 
3. Consider the design having four partitions as discussed earlier. The
operating frequency of the partition I is 500 MHz, partition II operates
at 333.33 MHz, partition III at 200 MHz, and the partition IV operates
at the frequency of 250 MHz. Other glue logic and buses operates at the
frequency of 150 MHz, and the design is partitioned into five clock
domains. In such circumstances, the design constraints to achieve the
required speed, power, and area are specified in the block-level
constraints.sdc file.
 
4. What exactly the synthesis tool does is, it uses the RTL design,
constraints and try to generate the gate-level netlist for these functional
blocks. The synthesis tools are intelligent enough as they go through
the various optimization phases to meet the constraints.
 
5. At the block level, if constraints are not met, then it is essential to
modify the RTL or tweak the architecture and it can have very good
impact on the later stages of the design.
 
6. This phase can even be helpful to understand and freeze the risks in the
design.  
Script 2: The Block-Level Synthesis Using Synopsys DC [1]

--- Page 184 ---
set active_design proceesor_functionality
analyze –format vhdl $active_design.vhd
elaborate $active_design
current_design $active_design
link
uniquify
set_wire_load_model –name SMALL
set_wire_load_mode top
set_operating_conditions WORST
create_clock –period 10 –waveform [list 0 5] matser_clk
set_clock_latency 1.0 [get_clocks master_clk]
set_clock_uncertainty –setup 2.0 [get_clocks master_clk]
set_clock_transition 0.1 [get_clocks tck]
set_dont_touch_network [list tck master_reset]
set_driving_cell –cell BUFF1X –pin Z [all_inputs]
set_drive 0 [list master_clk master_reset]
set_input_delay 2.0 –clock master_clk –max [all_inputs]
set_output_delay 2.0 –clock master_clk –max [all_outputs]
set_max_area 0
set_fix_multiple_port_nets –buffer_constants –all
compile –scan
check_test
remove_unconnected_ports [find –hierarchy cell {“*”}]
change_names –h –rules
set_dont_touch current_design
write–hierarchy –output $active_design.db
write–format vhdl –hierarchy –output $active_design.vhd
8.1.2 Chip-Level Synthesis and Constraints
At the top level, the design is integration of the different functional blocks,
IPs, and processors. The top-level constraints need to be derived depending
on the requirement of the speed, power, and area. At the initial stages of the
design cycle, during the architecture level, the information about this is
gathered and documented.
For example, my SOC design should work at 1 GHz operating
frequency and has the multiple clock domains. So, is it essential that every

--- Page 185 ---
functional block need to be operated at the 1 GHz? Answer is no! The
reason being the overall clocking rate to perform the single operation in one
cycle is 1 GHz and as the design have multiple functional blocks depending
on the partition, they can be operated individually at high speed or at low
speed also.
The architect and the design team should take care of the
synchronization for such designs. For the functional and timing proven IPs,
it can be possible to set the don’t touch attributes during the iterative phase
of synthesis and optimization.
Script 3: Top-Level Constraints Script Using Synopsys [1]
set active_design processor
set sub_modules {processor_logic decoding bus interface memory interrupt_control}
foreach module $sub_modules {
set syn_db $module.db
read_db syn_db
}
analyze –format vhdl $active_design.vhd
elaborate $active_design
current–design $active_design
link
uniquify
set_wire_load_model –name LARGE
set_wire_load_mode enclosed
set_operating_conditions WORST
create_clock – period 10 –waveform [list 0 5] master_clk
set_clock_latency 1.0 [get_clocks master_clk]
set_clock_uncertainty –setup 2.0 [get_clocks master_clk]
set_clock_transition 0.1 [get_clocks master_clk]
set_dont_touch_network [list master_clk master_reset]
set_driving_cell –cell BUFF1X –pinZ [all_inputs]
set_drive 0 [list master_clk master-reset]
set_input_delay 2.0–clock master_clk –max [all_inputs]
set_output_delay 2.0–clock master_clk –max [all_outputs]
set_max_area 0
set_fix_multiple_port_nets –all –buffer_constants

--- Page 186 ---
compile –scan
remove_attribute [find –hierarchy design {“*”}] dont_touch
current_design $active_design
uniquify
check_test
create_test_patterns –sample 5
preview_scan
insert_scan
check_test
compile –only_design_rule
remove_unconnected_ports [find –hierarchy cell {“*”}]
set_dont_touch current_design
write –hierarchy –output $active_design.db
write–format vhdl –hierarchy –output $active_design.vhd
8.2 Design Rule Constraints (DRC)
The main important design rule constraints are fanout, capacitance, and
transition. These constraints are having high priority during the synthesis as
compared to optimization constraints.
8.2.1 max_fanout
It is used to measure the number of loads a port or load can drive.
Consider the logic gate driving the multiple gates as shown in Fig. 8.2.

--- Page 187 ---
Fig. 8.2fanout of the cell
The technology library has information about the default fanout.
Designer can use the following command to get the fanout
get_attribute library_name default_fanout_load
8.2.2 max_transition
The maximum transition from ‘0’ to ‘1’ or from ‘1’ to ‘0’ for specific net or
entire design can be specified using Synopsys DC, as we know the
transition time is due to the RC time constant.
How DC meets the specified maximum transition?
Consider the library which specifies maximum transition of ‘4’ and designer
specifies max_transition of ‘2’. What DC does is that, it will try to meet the
max_transition of 2 (Fig. 8.3).

--- Page 188 ---
Fig. 8.3Transition from ‘0’ to ‘1’ and ‘1’ to ‘0’
The following Synopsys DC command is used to specify the
max_transition (Fig. 8.4).
Fig. 8.4Capacitance at driving cell
set_max_transition < value >< design_name/port_name >
8.2.3 max_capacitance
It is used to provide information about the maximum net capacitance.
During the compilation, the DC takes care about the violations due to
max_capacitance.

--- Page 189 ---
So, as we know the max_capacitance constraint at output of driving cell
is due to the net capacitance and the capacitance of pins driven by cell,
designer can use the following command to define max_capacitance.
set_max_capacitance < value >< port_name/design_name>
As discussed, use the following commands to define the design rule
constraints
set_max_transition < value >< design_name/port_name >
set_max_fanout < value >< port_name/design_name >
set_max_capacitance < value >< design_name/port_name>
8.3 Optimization Constraints
For the design optimization, during the logic synthesis, the DC uses the area
and speed constraints. Both the constraints should be met. The power
management and power constraints for ASIC design will be considered
during physical synthesis.
So, let us try to understand how the DC performs the optimization? Is it
that the DC will try to perform the optimization for the area and speed at the
same time? The answer is ‘no’, the reason being if I am the designer and I
wish to have the optimization then I will try to carry optimization of the
design to achieve desired speed. So, the speed optimization is carried out
first and then DC will optimize for the area.
As discussed in Chap. 7, DC uses few of the commands while
constraining the design and these commands are
create_clock
set_input_delay
set_output_delay
set_max_delay
set_min_delay
set_fix_hold
set_max_area
set_driving_cell

--- Page 190 ---
set_load
For the asynchronous path, the point-to-point delays are specified by the
following commands.
set_max_delay
set_min_delay
The following section discusses the DC commands used during the
optimization and during the performance improvement of the design.
8.3.1 set_dont_use
The command ‘set_dont_use’ can be used, if the synthesis engineer wish
not to refer the cells from the technology library.
If ‘don’t_touch’ attribute is used then this command ignores the cells
from the technology library.
The command is described below
set_dont_use library_name/cell_name
Consider cell name as XOR2 then during the optimization the XOR2
cell is not used.
8.3.2 set_dont_touch
Most of the time, during the optimization phase, we don’t need to optimize
the design which has already met the timing and area constraints. For
example, consider the design shown in the Fig. 8.5.

--- Page 191 ---
Fig. 8.5SOC top-level design diagram
If the functional design blocks such as processor, DSP logic, and
memory controller have met the timing and area constraints and as further
optimization is not required, the dont_touch command can be used.
The command is described below
set_dont_touch design_name
This we can prevent the synthesis reoptimization of the design
functionality.
During logic synthesis, we need to use the hand instantiated clock trees
and as DC doesn’t perform the synthesis for the clock tree, use the
don’t_touch attribute for the same.
So, if current design is soc_top, then we can do following to allow don’t
touch of the functional block
current_design = soc_top

--- Page 192 ---
set_dont_touch u1
set_dont_touch u2
set_dont_touch u3
Where the u1, u2, and u3 are the instances of the processor, DSP logic,
and memory controller, respectively.
8.3.3 set_prefer
This command is used when the synthesis engineer wish to change the
priority of cells chosen by the DC during technology translation.
Now, consider that the design netlist need to be mapped to another
technology library then the command can be used and is described below
set_prefer library_name/libaray_name
8.3.4 Command for the Design Flattening
We can have the hierarchical or flattened design. Now, consider the
following expressions used inside the architecture of VHDL design
Y1 <= a_in and b_in;
Y2 <= c_in and Y1;
The synthesis tool will infer the cascade logic and shown in the Fig. 8.6.
Fig. 8.6Gate-level structure without flattening
After flattening, we can get the expression as
Y2 <= (c_in and a_in) and (c_in and b_in);
set_flatten command is used to flatten the design.
The command is described as or may be referred as the switch
set_flatten true
If we refer the basic digital circuits, then the combinational logic is
expressed using sum of product (sop). The SOP expression will improve the

--- Page 193 ---
parallelism and the speed of the design.
Now let us think that can I use flattening for the multiplexers, XOR gates
and adders?
The answer is, it is not recommended to use flattening for this type of logic
due to the use of the control elements. During optimization, the DC will
never perform flattening unless and until it is specified.
Still there is always limitation to get the flattened netlist. It is
recommended that if the design has 10 or less number of inputs, then the
flatten switch can be used.
8.3.5 Commands Used for Structuring
If the objective is to improve the area or gate count, then structuring is
recommended.
The command is described below
set_structure –timing true
We can have the Boolean structuring or timing driven structuring. By
default, the DC uses the timing driven structuring. To execute the Boolean
structuring, we need to direct the synthesis tool.
8.3.6 Group and Ungroup Commands
To remove the hierarchy, the ‘ungroup’ command is used, and to create the
hierarchy, the ‘group’ command is used.
The command is described below
ungroup –flatten –all
This will allow ungrouping of all the levels below soc_top design. But
DC will take care that the design functionality for which the set_dont_touch
attribute was applied will not be disturbed.
To ungroup all the synthetic designs before compiling, the following
command can be used
replace_synthetic –ungroup
To group command can be used to have the new hierarchy. The command is
given below
Group (u1, u2) –design_name name_block –cell_name soc_top_inst

--- Page 194 ---
So as discussed the grouping forms the new instance soc_top_inst using the
instances u1, u2.
8.4 FSM Optimization
For the optimization of the finite state machines, the FSM compiler is used.
The use of FSM compiler is to achieve the area optimization with the
improved design performance. In the practical ASIC designs, the state
machine controller is designed as separate design block.
More details are given in the Chap. 6 about the Moore and Mealy state
machine.
The Script 4 can be used for the FSM extraction and optimization
Script 4: FSM Extraction Script
/* read the design object */
dc_shell > read -format vhdl state_machines_controller.vhd
/* Map the design */
dc_shell > compile –map_effort medium
/* if the design is not partitioned then group the logic */
dc_shell > set_fsm_state_vector {< flip_flop_name > , < flip_flop_name > ,…}
dc_shell > group –fsm –design_name < fsm_design_name>
/* extract the state machine from netlist in the state machine table format */
dc_shell > set_fsm_state_vector {< flip_flop_name > , < flip_flop_name > ,…}
dc_shell > set_fsm_encoding {“state0 = 0”, “state1 = 1”, …….}
dc_shell > extract
/* write the design in the FSM format */
dc_shell > write –format st –output state_machine.st
/* if the design is already in the state machine format then read the design */
dc_shell > read –format st state_machine.st
/* define the order of the state */
dc_shell > set_fsm_order {state0,state1,….}
/* define the encoding style */
dc_shell > set_fsm_encoding_style < encoding_style>
/* compile the design */
dc_shell > compile –map_effort high

--- Page 196 ---
8.5 Important Points to Conclude the Chapter
Following are important points to conclude the chapter
1. Synopsys DC uses the optimization constraints and design rule
constraints while performing synthesis.  
2. For complex designs, extract the chip-level, top-level, and block-level
constraints.  
3. The design rule constraints are specified for the fanout, transition, and
capacitance.  
4. Optimization constraints are specified for the area and speed. 
5. Synopsys DC does not optimize for the power. The power planning
need to be carried out during physical design.  
6. To report the area, use report_area and use report_timing to get the
timing report.  
7. Grouping is used to create new design hierarchy.  
8. Design flattening removes the hierarchy from the design. 
9. FSM optimization can be carried out using FSM compiler.  
This chapter has given us good understanding of the optimization and
design rule constraints. Even we have discussed about the DRC
commands and optimization commands. The next chapter will discuss
about the important ASIC synthesis optimization and performance
improvement strategies. Even the chapter will discuss about the area,
timing reports and scripts used for the setup and hold checks!
