9. Design Optimization Scenarios
Vaibbhav Taraate1 
 
1 Rupee S T (Semiconductor Training @ Rs. 1), Pune, Maharashtra,
India
 
Vaibbhav Taraate
Email: vaibbhavt@gmail.com
“Synopsys DC gives highest priority to optimize for timing as
compared to area optimization.”
Abstract
The chapter discusses the design optimization strategies to achieve the
speed and area constraints during synthesis using Synopsys DC. Most of the
time, we observe the timing violations and DRC violations. To optimize the
design to meet the constraints, the schemes which are used during synthesis
and optimization are discussed in this chapter. The chapter even discusses
the use of Synopsys DC commands to report the multicycle path and false
path and to perform the register balancing and register retiming.
KeywordsASIC – Synthesis – Optimization – False path – Setup – Hold –
Max area – STA – Timing paths – Multicycle paths – Constraints’ chip-level
constraints – Block-level constraints – FSM optimization – Area – Speed –
Power – Static power – Dynamic power – Group – Asynchronous paths –
Setup slack – Hold slack
9.1 Where Is the Issue in Optimization?

--- Page 199 ---
At the higher level, we can imagine that design constraints are not met for
the SOC design. There are many reasons behind this. What they may be?
1. The design logic density is so high that the top-level design is not able
to meet the area constraints.  
2. The design has many timing paths which are failing at the top level.
That is the design is not able to meet the timing constraints. 
3. The block-level design does not have any timing violation but when
integrated at the top level the top-level constraints are not met. 
4. The design at top level has only hold violations, but there is no any
issue for the setup time. Better to work on the setup violations during
prelayout STA and during physical design fix the hold violations.
 
5. My design is meeting all the constraints, but still there is chance to
improve the performance of my design.  
6. If we consider Fig. 9.1, then many times we can face issue of timing
and area for the processor interface with the memory controller and
interface of the video processor with the memory controller. The issue
may be due to the partitioning boundaries or pin-to-pin timing. Or the
partitioning is not proper as all they are in the different  clock groups.
 

--- Page 200 ---
Fig. 9.1SOC top-level design.
Note The discussion on analog design is out of scope for this book. The analog cluster
having different blocks is shown in Fig. 9.1
7. The issue of not meeting the power constraints in this book discussion
on the power constraints is out of scope.  
8. Another issue can be multicycle and false path reporting. If the
multicyle paths and false paths are not reported, then the optimization
is really a big issue.
 
9. If my design uses the state machine controller, consider the state
machine controller for initialization of the DDR3 controller or to
perform the pipelining and control in the general purpose processor. If
the optimization and constraints are not extracted at block level, then
it will be difficult to get the optimization and performance
improvement at the block as well as top level.
 

--- Page 201 ---
10.If my design has the intended latches, then the time borrowing and
working of the design for the active enable window need to be
considered.
 
In all such scenarios, the strategies need to be decided by the synthesis
and optimization team to achieve the clean timing and to meet the design
constraints. Let us have strategy to meet the timing first and then work on
the area constraints.
The design and optimization are vast topics, and I can document
hundreds of scenarios. But this chapter discusses few of the scenarios with
the optimization strategies and performance improvement techniques.
9.2 Design Optimization Strategies for the SOC
Now let us imagine as a synthesis team what should be our strategies during
the design and optimization for the large-density SOCs. The following are
few important points:
1. Have the initial synthesis run for the block-level design and report all
the violators  
2. Have closer look to the timing and area report and then try to apply for
the constraints at the block level. Report the all violated paths by using
the report_constraints –all_violators
 
3. If the improvement in the timing is requirement, then use the
asynchronous path grouping and then use the compilation strategies. 
4. If the large-density blocks have already met the timing then to prevent
the recompilation uses the ‘dont_touch’ attribute.  
5. If the area minimization is requirement, then use grouping, resource
sharing, and other techniques with the ‘set_max_area’ attribute.  
6. Report the multicycle paths in the design. Consider the SOC shown in
Fig. 9.1 which has processor engine and needs the floating point
multiplier. If the destination register is receiving the data after multiple
clock cycles, then it is essential to report the multicycle path. This will
 

--- Page 202 ---
be useful information to the timing analyzer to understand where to
check for the setup and where to check for the hold.
7. Report the false path for the block-level design.  
8. If block-level timing and area requirements are met for the design, then
use the top-level design constraints to optimize the top-level design. 
9. For the top-level optimization, let us imagine that the processor, video
processor, and memory controller individual timing are met but at the
top level there is an issue in meeting the constraints! Then what we
should do?
a. Check for the timing violation from the timing report, the setup
violation is due to the late arrival of the data. So try to tweak the
RTL to get the partition boundary clean.
 
b. If partition boundary is clean, then we can use the register
balancing techniques to eliminate the setup time violation. 
c. If the timing report indicates the hold violations, then it indicates
that at the block interface the data arrival is very fast so we can use
the strategies to append the buffers in the data path by taking care
that it will not have the setup time violation issue.
 
d. After all the possibilities if the timing is not met then try to tweak
the RTL, architecture or last option is to use the relaxed
constraints.
 
 
9.3 Compilation Strategies
We can choose either top-down or bottom-up compilation approach during
the design synthesis. Each compilation method has its own advantages and
disadvantages.
9.3.1 Top-Down Compilation

--- Page 203 ---
The top-down compilation uses the top-level design constraints. The
following are the advantages and disadvantages for the top-down
compilation
Advantages
1. Optimization engines work on full design, complete paths 
2. Usually get best optimization result  
3. No iteration required  
4. Simple constraints  
5. Simple data management.  
Disadvantages
1. Longer runtime  
2. More memory requirements 
3. More runtime.  
The commands used for the top-down compilation are
dc_shell> current_design TOP
dc_shell> compile –timing_high_effort_script
9.3.2 Bottom-up Compilation

--- Page 204 ---
The bottom-up compilation performs the submodule-level compilation first
and then it moves toward top level. The care must be taken by the designer
to set ‘set_dont_touch’ attribute on the submodules to avoid recompilation of
the submodules. The designer needs to know the timing information for the
inputs and outputs for each of the submodule. The advantages and
disadvantages are discussed below.
Advantages
1. Faster as compared to top-down compilation 
2. Less processing required per run  
3. Less memory requirement.  
Disadvantages
1. Optimization works on the submodule or subdesign 
2. More iteration required  
3. More hierarchies to maintain.  
Consider the design has two submodules. The commands used for the
bottom-up compilation are
dc_shell> current_design submodule1
dc_shell> compile –timing_high_effort_script
dc_shell> set_dont_touch submodule1
dc_shell> current_design submodule2
dc_shell> compile –timing_high_effort_script
dc_shell> set_dont_touch submodule2
dc_shell> current_design TOP

--- Page 205 ---
dc_shell> compile –timing_high_effort_script
9.4 Strategies for Area Optimization
There are several techniques used for minimizing the overall area of the
design. The highest priority of the designer is to optimize for the timing and
then for the area. There are several efficient area minimization techniques at
the RTL level. In the previous few chapters, we have discussed resource
sharing using RTL tweaks. This section will give more information about
the area optimization and techniques using Synopsys DC. The following are
the important guidelines used to optimize for the area.
1. Avoid the use of the combinational logic as individual block or design. 
2. Do not use the glue logic between two designs.  
3. Use set_max_area attribute while synthesizing the design.  
9.4.1 Do not Use Combinational Logic as Individual Block
It is recommended that avoid the use of the combinational logic as
individual block. If the individual combinational design block is used, then
Design Compiler will not be able to optimize the individual block. This is
not a good design partitioning. The hierarchy of the module is fixed, and
Design Compiler will not be able to modify the hierarchy of the design.
Consider the scenario shown in Fig. 9.2. It has module I and module II. As
shown the module II is separate combinational block so the Design
Compiler will not be able to optimize module II, the reason being the
Design Compiler doesn’t optimize the port interfaces.

--- Page 206 ---
Fig. 9.2Combinational logic as individual module [2]
If the design is partitioned by considering the boundaries, then the
overall optimization will boost the design performance. A better partitioned
ASIC design should have combined functionality of module I and module
II. The functionality of A + B in the single module I is shown in Fig. 9.3 and
results in the faster optimization for the design.
Fig. 9.3Eliminating individual combinational module
9.4.2 Do not Use Glue Logic Between Two Modules
If the module II in Fig. 9.3 is replaced by glue logic that is instance of logic
gate, then it glues between the different modules and shown in Fig. 9.4.
Such type of design partitioning is not good, the reason being the logic gate
cannot be optimized by the Design Compiler as design is not partitioned

--- Page 207 ---
properly. To avoid this type of scenario, it is recommended to use the group
command. Either group the glue logic in the module I or module II.
The following command is used to group the glue logic into module II
(Fig. 9.4).
Fig. 9.4Glue logic between two blocks
dc_shell> group {m2, m2} –design_name module III cell_name nand_gate
9.4.3 Use of set_max_area Attribute
To obtain the minimum possible area, it is recommended to use the attribute
set_max_area. This attribute is effective during the optimization of the
design. Design Compiler gives the highest priority to the timing
optimization. If timing is met, then only the area optimization phase can
start. The priorities for the design optimization are DRC, timing, power, and
then area.

--- Page 208 ---
9.4.4 Area Report
The area report is generated by the Design Compiler using report_area
command. The sample area report is shown in Report 1. The area report for
any design consists of the number of ports, nets, references. It also gives
information about the combinational, sequential, and total cell area. The
fields of the area report are listed below
Report 1:Area report fields [2]
Number of ports:
Number of nets:
Number of cells:
Number of references:
Combinational area:
Non-combinational area:
Net interconnect area: undefined (Wire load has zero net area)
Total cell area:
Total area:
9.5 Strategies for Timing Optimization and
Performance Improvement
While optimization, the timing has highest priority as compared to the
power and area. We are not using Synopsys DC for the power optimization.
Power planning will be done during later stage using physical compiler.
During the first phase of optimization, the Design Compiler checks for the
design rule constraint (DRC) violations, then the timing violations and the
power constraints and finally the area constraints. This section discusses the
few timing optimization commands supported by the Design Compiler.
9.5.1 Design Compilation with ‘map_effort_high’
Most of the time, design engineer uses the option as map_effort medium
while performing the synthesis. It is advisable that during synthesis of the
first phase, designer can use the option as map_effort medium as it reduces
the compilation time. If the deign constraints are not met, then the designer

--- Page 209 ---
can go for the incremental compilation with the option as map_effort high.
This can improve the design performance by at least 5–10%.
The sdc command is shown below.
dc_shell> compile –map_effort_high –incremental_mapping
9.5.2 Use of group_path Command
The design performance can boost up to 10% by using the map_effort high
option. But if timing is not met with the incremental compilation by using
the design constraints, then it is essential to group the critical timing paths
and use the weight factor to boost the design performance. This command is
useful to improve the timing performance. The command is shown below.
dc_shell> group_path –name critical1 –from <input_name>–to <output_name>–
weight <weight factor>
Consider the design scenario which has the setup violation of 0.38 ns.
The setup violation is difference between the data required time and data
arrival time. So the slack is negative and setup time is violated.
dc_shell> read –format vhdl combinational_design.vhd
dc_shell> create_clock –name clk –period 15
dc_shell> set_input_delay 3 –clock clk in_a
dc_shell> set_input_delay 3 –clock clk in_b
dc_shell> set_input_delay 3 –clock clk c_in
dc_shell> set_output_delay 3 –clock c_out
dc_shell> current_design = combinational_design
dc_shell> compile –map_effort medium
dc_shell> report_timing –path full –delay max –max_path 1 –nworst 1
After the successful design synthesis use the report_timing command
while performing the timing analysis. The timing report for the design can

--- Page 210 ---
be obtained with the multiple options as shown in the above script and
shown in Report 2.


--- Page 211 ---
Report 2Timing report with negative slack
To fix the setup violation and to boost the design performance, the designer
can use the group_path with the weight factor. More the weight factor more
is the compilation time.
dc_shell> group_path –name critical1 –from c_in –to c_out –weight 5
dc_shell> compile –map_effort high –incremental mapping
dc_shell> report_timing –path full –delay max –max_path 1 –nworst 1
As shown in the timing report the design has positive slack as it remove
setup violation and shown in Report 3.

--- Page 212 ---
Report 3Timing report with the positive slack

--- Page 213 ---
As shown in the timing Report 3 for the max analysis with the
compile_map high option, due to weight factor of 5 the slack is met.
9.5.3 Setup Time Analysis and Check
The setup slack is difference between the data required time (RT) and data
arrival time (AT). If setup slack is met means the difference between the RT
and AT is positive. The script using the Synopsys PT commands for setup
time check is described in Script 1.
Script 1: Setup check [1]
set active_design soc_top
read_db –netlist_only $active_design.db
current_design $active_design
set_wire_load_model –name large
set_wire_load_mode top
set_operating_conditions WORST
set_load 40.0 [all_outputs]
set_driving_cell –cell BUFF1X –pinZ [all_inputs]
create_clock –period 10 –waveform [0 5] master_clk
set_clock_latency 1.0 [get_clocks master_clk]
set_clock_transition 0.1 [get_clocks master_clk]
set_clock_uncertainty 2.0 –setup [get_clocks master_clk]
set_input_delay 2.0 –clock master_clk [all_inputs]
set_output_delay 2.0 –clock master_clk [all_outputs]
report_constraint –all_violators
report_timing –to [all_registers –data_pins]
report_timing –to [all_outputs]
write_sdf–context vhdl –output $active_design.sdf
The sample timing report is shown in Report 4. As shown, the difference
between the data required time and data arrival time is positive. This
indicates no timing violation due to positive slack.

--- Page 214 ---
Report 4Timing report for the setup analysis [1].
If slack is not met, then what need to do?
1. Consider the late arrival signals in the data path. 
2. Tweak the RTL.  
3. Tweak the architecture micro-architecture. 
9.5.4 Hold Time Analysis and Check
Hold slack is defined as difference between the data arrival time and data
required time. The slack should be positive that means during the hold time

--- Page 215 ---
window, there is no any transition. The script using the Synopsys PT
commands for hold time check is described in Script 2.
Script 2: Hold time check [1].
set active_design soc_top
read_db –netlist_only $active_design.db
current_design $active_design
set_wire_load large
set_wire_load_mode top
set_operating_conditions BEST
set_load 50.0 [all_outputs]
set_driving_cell –cell BUFF1X –pin Z [all_inputs]
create_clock –period 10 –waveform [0 5] master_clk
set_clock_latency 1.0 [get_clocks master_clk]
set_clock_transition 0.1 [get_clocks master_clk]
set_clock_uncertainty 0.5 –hold [get_clocks master_clk]
set_input_delay 0.0–clock master_clk [all_inputs]
set_output_delay 0.0–clock master_clk[all_outputs]
report_constraint –all_violators
report_timing –to [all_registers –data_pins] –delay_type min
report_timing –to [all_outputs] –delay_type min
write_sdf -context vhdl–output $active_design.sdf
The sample timing report is shown in Report 5. As shown, the difference
between the data arrival time and data required time is positive. This
indicates no timing violation due to positive slack.

--- Page 216 ---
Report 5Timing report for the hold analysis [1].
If slack is not met, then what need to do
1. Consider the early arrival signals in the data path. 
2. Tweak the RTL.  
3. Tweak the architecture, micro-architecture.  
9.5.5 Submodule Characterization
In the practical ASIC designs, the design can have multiple hierarchies.
Consider that the top-level design consists of submodules X, Y, Z. If
independently these submodules are synthesized and optimized for the
design constraints, they might meet the timing requirements independently.
When these submodules are instantiated in the higher level of hierarchy top,

--- Page 217 ---
then it may be possible that they do not meet the timing. The reason for this
may be the glue logic used in between the submodules X, Y, Z or the tight
constraints at the top-level hierarchy.
Under such circumstances to meet the design constraints, it is advisable
to use the characterize command. This command allows the capturing of
the boundary conditions for the submodule based on the top-level hierarchy
environment. Each submodule can be compiled and characterize
independently.
The following is the small script which can enable the characterization
of the individual submodules. Consider the submodule X, Y, Z instance
names as I1, I2, and I3
dc_shell> current_design = TOP
dc_shell> characterize I1
dc_shell> compile –map_effort high –incremental mapping
dc_shell> current_design = TOP
dc_shell> characterize I2
dc_shell> compile –map_effort high –incremental mapping
dc_shell> current_design = TOP
dc_shell> characterize I3
dc_shell> compile –map_effort high –incremental mapping
dc_shell> current_design = TOP
9.5.6 Register Balancing
Register balancing is very efficient and powerful technique to move the
combinational logic from one pipelined stage to another pipelined stage.
This technique improves the design performance by moving the logic and
hence reduces the register to register delay. Consider the pipelined design
shown in Fig. 9.5 and consisting of the three flip-flops and combinational
logic. In the first pipelined stage, the combinational logic is 4-variable
function and the second pipelined stage has combinational logic as 8-
variable function and has more propagation delay as compared to the 4-
variable combinational logic. Due to the different propagation delay in two
different pipelined stages, the design performance is decided by the register
to register timing path which has more delay.

--- Page 218 ---
Fig. 9.5Pipelined stages
Under such circumstances, the register balancing can be used to shift
the combinational logic from one of the pipelined stages to another
pipelined stage without affecting the functionality of the design. This is
achieved by compiler by using the following set of commands.
dc_shell> balance_registers
dc_shell> report_timing –path full –delay max –max_path 1 –nworst 1
9.5.7 Multicycle Paths
The multicycle paths in the design need to be reported. The paths can be set
so that the timing analyzer can perform the setup and hold check (Fig. 9.6).
Fig. 9.6Multicycle path
The commands used to set the multicycle path are listed in Table 9.1.
Table 9.1Commands used to report multicycle path
Command Description
create_clock –name clk_master -period 5
[get_ports clk_master]
To create the master clock of period 5 nsec

--- Page 219 ---
Command Description
set_multicycle_path 3 –setup –from [get_pins
UFFO/Q] –to [get_pins UFF1/D]
This set the multicycle path of 3 cycles
set_multicycle_path 2 –hold –from [get_pins
UFFO/Q] –to [get_pins UFF1/D]
This is used to move the hold check by 2 clock
cycles as set up is checked at third clock cycles
Consider the design scenario for the processor block. The processor
needs to perform the multiplication instruction and has pipelined
architecture. As multiplication requires more number of cycle the data
launched by the FF1 to FF2 is three cycle path, In such kind of scenarios
the setup need to be pushed by three cycles and need to move the hold
check by 2 cycles.

--- Page 220 ---
9.6 Important Points to Conclude the Chapter
The following are important points to conclude the chapter.
1. Use the initial synthesis run and try to find the all violators during the
block-level synthesis.  
2. If block-level timing constraints are met, then only try to optimize for
the area if it is requirement.  
3. To optimize for area use the set_max_area attribute.  
4. To report the area use report_area and to report the timing use
report_timing  
5. To report all violators, use the command report_cosntraints –
all_violators  
6. Timing constraints are met indicates the positive setup and hold slack. 
7. To optimize the design for the timing use compile, characterize with
weight factor.  
8. Use the required area and speed optimization techniques to meet the
block and chip-level timing constraints.  
9. Report the multicycle and false paths for the design.  
This chapter has given us good understanding of the optimization
strategies and scenarios at the block level and chip level. In the next
chapter, we will discuss why the FPGA is best candidate for the
prototype? Even the next chapter will discuss the FPGA architecture and
Xilinx 7 series FPGAs.

“High-density FPGAs from Xilinx and Intel are best candidates for the SOCprototyping.”
AbstractThis chapter discusses the FPGA architecture, FPGA design flow and how to utilizeFPGA resources for the SOC prototyping. The chapter discusses the FPGA synthesisand how the logic is inferred using the FPGA design tools. The VHDL design withfew scenarios and how to tweak the RTL for the FPGA design is also discussed in thechapter for better understanding of the FPGA synthesis.
KeywordsFPGA – SOC – Spartan-3A – Artix-7 – LUT – CLB – Slice – BRAM –Multiplier – Synthesis – RTL – VHDL – Implementation – Memory – DistributedRAM – Clocking – PLL – Decoding logic – Resource sharing – Logic duplication
10.1 Xilinx 7 Series FPGA
The objective of this section is to have the important highlights about the Xilinx 7series FPGA. Subsequently, we will try to use the Artix-7 board to understand theSOC prototype flow and how to get the efficient prototype using Xilinx Vivado tool.
Xilinx 7 series FPGA architecture highlights:
(1)It has the unified architecture and operates at 1 V for the technology library of28 nm.  
(2)The FPGA architecture has blocks CLBs, IOBs, transceivers, DSPs, PCIe,ADCs, and clock management tiles (CMT) .  

--- Page 223 ---
(3)The mid-range series of Xilinx 7 series architecture is Kintex and low end isArtix.  
(4)If we consider the high-end Virtex family architecture, then the architecture hasthe 3D multi-die stacked silicon interconnects (SSI), up to 1200 user pin IOs, upto 8 MB RAM, up to 2 M logic cells and 2.4 M flip-flops.
 
For more information, please visit www. xilinx. com for the architecture andpackage information of Artix-7 FPGAs. Figure 10.1 gives information about thedifferent functional block in Xilinx 7 series architecture.
Fig. 10.1Xilinx 7 series resources
Important features of the Xilinx 7 series FPGA are shown in Fig. 10.2 and the keyresources are CLB, BRAMs, DSP blocks, clocking resources, IO blocks, in-built IPs,routing and interconnect resources, transceiver, and system monitor features. Thissection discusses them with few design scenarios which can be useful during theprototyping.

--- Page 224 ---
Fig. 10.2Xilinx 7 series FPGA architecture blocks
10.1.1 Xilinx 7 Series CLB Architecture
The Xilinx 7 series CLB is shown in Fig. 10.3, and as shown, the following areimportant highlights

--- Page 225 ---
Fig. 10.3Xilinx 7 series CLB architecture [1]
1.It has two slices per CLB, and each CLB consists of six-input LUTs.  
2.Two slices are named as SLICEM and SLICEL; SLICEM consists of RAM/SR,and SLICEL consists of only logic.  
3.It has four flip-flops or latches and can be configured depending on the bit streamof the functionality.  
4.The CLB architecture has wide multiplexers and carry chain logic. So thearchitecture of CLB is efficient enough to design six inputs and multiple outputcombinational or sequential logic.
 
Example 1 below gives information about the use of CLBs? The synthesis toolwill infer the logic using multiple adders, subtractors, and required logic gates forsuch kind of design. The multiplexer logic is used to generate one of the outputsdepending on the unique code for the instruction which is called as opcode.
Example 1: VHDL description for the 8-bit ALU
Library ieee;

--- Page 226 ---
Use ieee.std_logic_1164.all;
use ieee.std_logic_unsigned.all;
use ieee.std_logic_arith.all;
Entity  alu_logic is 
Port (  clk, reset_n: in std_logic;
op_code : in std_logic_vector ( 3 downto 0);
 a_in,b_in : in std_logic_vector ( 15 downto 0);
cin : in std_logic;
 alu_out : out std_logic_vector (15 downto 0);
 cout: out std_logic);
end alu_logic;
architecture arch_alu of alu_logic is
begin
         process (clk, reset_n, op_code, a_in, b_in, cin)
         variable result_out : unsigned (16 downto 0);
         begin
                 if (reset_n=‘0’) then
                 result_out :=(others =>‘0’);
                 else
                 case (op_code) is
                 when ”0000”=>  result_out := unsigned (‘0’ & a_in);
          when ”0001” => result_out := unsigned (‘0’ &  a_in) +unsigned (‘0’ & b_in);
                 when ”0010” => result_out := unsigned (‘0’ &  a_in) +unsigned (‘0’ & b_in)+cin;
              when ”0011” =>result_out :=  unsigned (‘0’ &  a_in) - unsigned (‘0’ & b_in);
                 when ”0100” => result_out := unsigned (‘0’ &  a_in) - unsigned (‘0’ & b_in)- cin;
                 when ”0101” =>result_out :=  unsigned (‘0’ &  a_in) +’1’;
                 when ”0110” => result_out := unsigned (‘0’ &  a_in) -’1’;
                 when ”1000” => result_out := unsigned (‘0’ & (a_in OR  b_in));
                 when ”1001” => result_out := unsigned (‘0’ & (a_in XOR  b_in));
                 when ”1010”  => result_out := unsigned (‘0’ & (a_in AND  b_in));
                 when ”1011”  => result_out := unsigned (‘0’ & NOT  (a_in ));

--- Page 227 ---
                 when others => result_out := (others=>‘0’);
                 end case; 
                 end if; 
         alu_out<= std_logic_vector( result_out(15 downto 0));
         cout <= std_logic(result_out(16));
         end process;
end  arch_alu;
The synthesis result is shown in Fig. 10.4. Still there is chance to optimize the designusing the resource sharing concept.
Fig. 10.4Synthesis result for the 8-bit ALU
10.1.2 Xilinx 7 Series Block RAM
The BRAMs are required in many applications, and the architecture of BRAM isvendor dependent. They can be configured using vendor-dependent EDA tool for therequired memory configuration. The following are important highlights for the Xilinx7 series BRAM1.The architecture has 36 KB BRAM and which can be visualized as 2 × 18 KBBRAM.  

--- Page 228 ---
2.The BRAM is synchronous RAM and can be cascaded without any logicoverheads to get 64 K × 1.  
3.The BRAM can be used as single port and dual port. In the dual-port mode, the18 KB BRAM can be used and configured as 18K × 1, 9K × 2, 8K × 4, 4K × 9,etc., and 36 KB BRAM can be used as 1K × 36, 2K × 9, 4K × 9, etc.
 
4.The BRAM architecture has built-in error correction (64-bit ECC), and they canbe used also in FIFO mode (Fig. 10.5).
Fig. 10.5Xilinx 7 series BRAM [1]
 

--- Page 229 ---
All kinds of the SOC design uses the memories of type RAM, ROM, contentaddressable. So let us think that, how these kinds of memories can be implemented.Either the memories can be instantiated from the cell library or from memorygenerator.To implement the small memories of few bits, the LUTs can be used. It isimportant that these memories can be efficient enough to load, store, and pass thedata. But to have the better and efficient architecture for the design instead ofdistributing the memories over the FPGA fabric, it is always better to use the BRAMs.The main features of BRAM are:
1.Synchronous memory: BRAMs can implement the synchronous single- or dual-port memory. One of the real beauties of such memory blocks is that, whenconfigured as dual-port RAM, each port can operate at different clockfrequencies.
 
2.They can be configured: The BRAM block is dedicated dual-port synchronousRAM block and can be configured as discussed above. Each port can beconfigured independently.
 
3.BRAMs and their use in the FIFO designs: The BRAMs can be used to storethe data as they are dedicated memory blocks and can be configured. Withadditional logic, FIFOs can be implemented using BRAMs. The depth of theFIFOs can be configured with the restriction that both the read and write sidesshould have same width.
 
4.Error correction: Consider the BRAM is configured as the 64-bit RAM, andthen, each BRAM can store additional Hamming code bits. These bits are used toperform the single-bit and double-bit error corrections during the read process.For 64-bit BRAM, each BRAM can store the eight-bit Hamming code. The errorcorrection logic can be also used while writing or reading from the externalmemories.
 
So let us think how the BRAMs are inferred?
The synthesis tool partitions the larger memories into small blocks, and each blockcan be implemented using BRAM. Effectively in the simple words, the BRAMs arevery effective building block which are inferred automatically during synthesis andthey are organised to implement the wide range of memories used for the SOC.Example 2 describes 16 × 2 BRAM and has 4 address lines and 2-bit data inputand 2-bit data output. It uses additional control signals as ‘clk’ and ‘write_en’.
Example 2: VHDL description for 16 × 2 BRAM

--- Page 230 ---
library  ieee; 
use ieee.std_logic_1164.all;
use ieee.std_logic_unsigned.all;
entity BRAM_16x2 is
port (q_out: out std_logic_vector(1 downto 0);
write_en : in std_logic;
clk: in std_logic;
d_in: in std_logic_vector(1 downto 0);
a_in: in std_logic_vector(3 downto 0));
end BRAM_16x2;
architecture BRAM_arch of BRAM_16x2 is
component RAM16x1S is
port (O : out std_logic;
D : in std_logic;
A3, A2, A1, A0 : in std_logic;
WE, WCLK : in std_logic); 
end component;
Begin
U0 : RAM16x1S
port map (O => q_out(0), WE => write_en, WCLK => clk, D=> d_in(0), A0 => a_in(0), A1 => a_in(1), A2
=> a_in(2), A3 => a_in(3));
U1 : RAM16x1S
port map (O => q_out(1), WE => write_en, WCLK => clk, D=> d_in(1), A0 => a_in(0), A1 => a_in(1), A2
 => a_in(2),A3 => a_in(3));

--- Page 231 ---
end BRAM_arch;
The synthesis result for the BRAM 16 × 2 inferred by Vivado is shown in Fig. 10.6.
Fig. 10.6Synthesis result for the 16 × 2-bit BRAM
10.1.3 Xilinx 7 Series DSP
The Xilinx 7 series DSP48E1 slice architecture is shown in Fig. 10.7, and it supports96-bit multiply accumulate (MACC) operation. Few of the highlights of DSP blockare

--- Page 232 ---
Fig. 10.7Xilinx 7 series DSP slice [1]
(1)DSP slice has 25-bit pre-adder, 25 × 18-bit signed multiplier, and 48-bit ALU. 
(2)It is mainly used to implement the DSP algorithms; the shifters can beimplemented by using 17-bit shifter and pattern detector.  
(3)The slice also has cascade paths to implement a wide range of DSP functions andalgorithms.  
(4)It also provides the implementation of pipelined logic for the 12/24 bit of thedata.  
Example 3 describes the multiplier implementation using DSP block. Thesynthesis tool will try to push the logic into DSP block for such kind of designs.
Example 3: VHDL RTL for the 32-bit multiplier
library ieee;
use ieee.std_logic_1164.all;
use ieee.std_logic_unsigned.all;
use ieee.std_logic_arith.all;
entity without_resource_sharing is
port (a_in,b_in,c_in,d_in: in std_logic_vector (31 downto 0);
sel_in: in std_logic;
y_out: out std_logic_vector ( 63 downto 0));

--- Page 233 ---
end without_resource_sharing;
--Functional Description without pushing the common resources 
architecture arch_without_sharing ofwithout_resource_sharingis
begin
--Process sensitive to all the inputs and control signals
process (a_in, b_in, c_in, d_in, sel_in)
begin
          if (sel_in=‘1’) then
          y_out <= a_in *  b_in;
          else
          y_out <= c_in *  d_in;
          end if;
end process; 
end arch_without_sharing;
This kind of the design infers the multiplier chain at the input and depending on thestatus of the select input result of one of the multipliers is assigned to output (Fig.10.8). Using resource sharing, the single multiplier can be used and it is discussed inSect. 10.3.
Fig. 10.8Synthesis outcome of the 32 × 32-bit multiplier
Figure 10.9 gives information about the device utilization. The inferred logic usesthe four DSPs which is just 4.44% utilization of the available DSP blocks.

--- Page 234 ---
Fig. 10.9Artix-7 device utilization for multiplier
10.1.4 Xilinx 7 Series Clocking
For any design, the performance is dependent on the clock generation logic andarchitecture used for clocking the multiple CLBs. The clocking architecture for theXilinx 7 series devices is shown in Fig. 10.10, and the following are few of theimportant points

--- Page 235 ---
Fig. 10.10Xilinx 7 series clocking [1]
(1)The clock is divided into the clock regions, and effectively, it is half-width forevery 50 CLBs.  
(2)Every clock region can be treated as group of 20 DSPs, 10 BRAMs, 4transceivers, 50 IOBs, and PCIe.  
(3)It has CMTs, and the tiles are used adjacent to IO columns that is 1 tile perregion and 2 columns per device.  
(4)It has one centre clock spine and that is 1 horizontal clock row/region. 
10.1.5 XILINX 7 Series IO
As discussed in the earlier section, the overall performance of the SOC is dependenton the available IOs and their bandwidth the data transfer. The following areimportant points(1)The high-range IOBs supports standard up to 3.3 V, and the high-performanceIOB supports the standard up to 1.8 V.  
(2)The Xilinx 7 series has various types of IOs, and they are listed in Table 10.1:Xilinx 7 series IOs.  

--- Page 236 ---
Table 10.1Xilinx 7 series IOs
Sr.no. IO Description
1 Input–output block(IOB) The architecture has 2 columns per device and 50 IOBs per bank. Two distinctIOB types are high range and high performance
2 High-speed serialIO transceiverTransceivers are available in quad that is 4 per block. They have differenttypes and multiple standards: GTH/GTP/GTX/GTZ with the bandwidth of3.75–28 Gbps
3 PCI Express(PCIe) blocksThey are built in GTX serial IO transceivers, and they are compatible with theGen 1,2,3 protocols having 2.5, 5, and 8 Gbps bandwidth
4 XADCs For every analog-to-digital converter, there is analog sensor and thearchitecture has 12-bit two ADCs
10.1.6 Xilinx 7 Series Transceivers
The architecture has the low-power gigabit transceiver. Due to low-powerarchitecture, the chip-to-chip interface is optimized and this is one of the powerfulfeatures of this FPGA. The following are few highlights regarding the performance ofthese transceivers.
(1)The high-performance transceiver is capable to support the data rate from 6.6 to28.05 Gb/s depending on the family of the Virtex 7 FPGA.  
(2)The transceiver count is 16 in the Artix-7 FPGA family, up to 32 transceivers inthe Kintex 7 family and up to 96 transceivers in the Virtex 7 family.  
(3)To improve the IP portability, the architecture of the serial transceiver uses thering oscillators and LC tank circuit.  
(4)The transmitter and receiver circuits are different, and they uses the PLL tomultiply the reference clock by the programmable number up to 100 to get thebit-serial clock.
 
10.1.6.1 Transmitter
Following are the key features of gigabit transmitter:
1.The transmitter is parallel-to-serial converter with conversion ratio of 16, 20, 32,40, 64, or 80.  
2.The GTZ transmitter supports up to 160-bit data width. 

--- Page 237 ---
3.It uses TXOUTCLK and used to register the parallel data. 
4.The incoming parallel data is fed through an optional FIFO, and it provide thesufficient number of transitions, it has additional support of 8B/10B, 64B/66Bencoding schemes.
 
5.The output of these transmitters drives the PC board with the single-channeldifferential output signal.  
6.To compensate for the PC board losses, the output signal pair has programmablesignal swing.  
7.To reduce the power consumption, this swing can be reduced for the shorterchannels.  
10.1.6.2 Receiver
Following are key features of gigabit receivers:
1.The receiver is serial-to-parallel converter with conversion ratio of 16, 20, 32, 40,64, or 80.  
2.The GTZ receiver supports up to 160-bit data width.  
3.To guarantee sufficient data transition, it uses non-return-to-zero (NRZ) encoding. 
4.The parallel data is transferred into the FPGA using the RXUSRCLK. 
5.For short channels to reduce power consumption by almost 30%, the transceiveroffers special low-power (LPM) mode.  
10.1.7 In-Built IPs
Nowadays, FPGAs are more capable to be used in the communication, medicalimaging, and networking areas due to available PCIe, Ethernet MAC, Phy, andprocessor cores on the FPGA fabric.The Xilinx Virtex 7 series FPGA has the PCIe, Ethernet MAC, Phy integrated onthe FPGA fabric.
Ethernet: It can operate with the speed of 2.5 Gbits/s, and the Ethernet block isdesigned with the IEEE standard 802.3-2005
– The four tri-mode (10/100/1000 Mb/s) MAC blocks can be connected to theFPGA fabric, transceivers.

--- Page 238 ---
PCIe: The FPGA has the integrated interface block for the PCI Express and can beconfigured as the endpoint. It can operate at the speed of the 2.5 and 5 Gbit/s datarate.CPU hard core: The optimized ARM IP core which can run at the higher speed (10times more) as compared to RTL, and it can be used during prototyping if thee SOCprocessor core features are matching with the available IP core. If multipleprocessors are required during the prototyping, then use the multiple FPGAs withthe partitioned logic, but this may reduce the performance of the prototype due tothe bus access time. These blocks are not directly inferred by synthesis tool butneed to be instantiated using core generators.
10.1.8 Built-In Monitor
It can be used during prototyping to get the thermal and power supply information,and it does not need any instantiation in the design. Once the power connections aremade, then using the Test Access Port (TAP), the data can be accessed at any time.
10.2 Synthesis and Implementation Flow [2]
Synthesis during the prototype cycle can be performed before or after designpartitioning. The synthesis is the process of converting the RTL into equivalent gate-level netlist. If we consider the ASICs, then the synthesis is the process to get thenetlist using the standard cells for the targeted process node. In case of FPGA,outcome of the synthesis is the netlist using the FPGA resources. More about theFPGA architecture and prototype using single and miltiple FPGAs  is discussed inChap. 11.Depending on the target FPGA family or device, the synthesis tool generates theFPGA netlist and the FPGA backend tool uses the design constraints and netlist duringimplementation. Most of the time during the prototype, we need to understand aboutthe expected speed or performance for the design and that can be accomplished bysynthesis tools and it can be useful during the early phase of the design cycle totweak the architectures and constraints to achieve the required performance.For the FPGA design, the design flow from the RTL to device programming isdescribed in Fig. 10.11.

--- Page 239 ---
Fig. 10.11 FPGA design flow
RTL design for the SOC prototype is one of the major milestones, and in thismilestone, the RTL is tweaked to fit into the target FPGA. This may involve thechanges in the IPs, clock resources, and resizing the memory blocks, etc.The design partitioning is required if the design specifications are complex anddesign does not fit in the single FPGA. If multiple FPGAs are needed for theprototype and test the design, then the design can be partitioned manually or by usingthe partitioning tools. The design partitioning is discussed in detail in the next fewchapters.For the implementation tool, we need to provide the constraints and theseconstraints are to define the required speed and pin placements. These can be used bythe backend tools to optimize the design. Although the vendor-dependent directivesand algorithms at the synthesis level can optimize the design to achieve the desiredperformance, but it is essential to use the implementation constraints.A place and route tool use the FPGA netlist and the design constraints to place andmap the design for the target FPGA. The FPGA bit-stream file will be loaded into the

--- Page 240 ---
FPGA to achieve the desired functionality. Although it looks simple, this processinvolves multiple steps such as mapping, placement, routing, and timing analysis.The following can be few of the backend tools useful while prototyping the SOC
1.Floorplanning tool: Consider I need to place hundreds of IOs to achieve theperformance then what I should do? I will use the floorplanning tool which canachieve the IO placement, and for few critical designs it can be useful to place thelogic to meet the performance.
 
2.Core generators: This kind of tool is used to infer the IP cores for the specificRTL. The objective and use of the core generator are to understand the RTLstructures and replace them by the existing IP cores available for that FPGA. Inlayman’s language, it is like replacing the RTL with its FPGA equivalent logic.
 
3.FPGA editors: This can be used to modify the FPGA design after the placementand routing. This can be used as debugging tool at the implementation levelwhich allows the designer to modify the FPGA block placements, routing, etc.
 
4.In-circuit debugging tool: This allows the designer to capture and view theinternal design nodes.  
Now let us focus on the important practical aspect about how the logic is mappedinside the FPGA? (more details in Chap. 11, 12)
10.2.1 How Logic Is Mapped Using CLBs?
The logic is mapped using the CLBs as each CLB consists of multiple input LUTs andregisters. If we consider the Virtex7 architecture, then for the six-input logic functionlogic is mapped using 6-input LUTs. For more number of inputs, the LUTs can becascaded, and for the less number of input variables, the LUTs will be splited torealize the function.Consider the practical scenario where 4 or 5 inputs are used by the logic function,and then it can map the logic inside one CLB.Shift register or distributed RAM is inferred using the SLICEM.If we use the efficient FPGA synthesis tool, then it can detect the possibility of theset/reset assertions for asynchronous and synchronous set and reset to avoid themalfunctioning inside the FPGA.
10.2.2 How DSP Blocks Are Mapped?
The Virtex7 has the dedicated DSP block, and to improve the FPGA performance andto avoid the use of the other functional blocks, the synthesis tool can map the DSPfunctionality using the dedicated DSP blocks.Consider the multiply and accumulate (MAC) function, this can be mapped usingthe DSP block for the improved performance. If the DSP algorithm is more complex,

--- Page 241 ---
then multiple DSP blocks can be used to implement the wider range of arithmeticalgorithms. If we wish to have the pipelining in the DSP algorithm, then the pipelinedlogic can be mapped by using the DSP blocks.
10.2.3 How Memory Blocks Are Mapped Inside FPGA?
We need to have the internal storage in the form of the distributed RAM or blockRAMs. The performance of the overall design depends upon the mapping of theseblocks efficiently by synthesis tool. What we need to look into is that whether thesynthesis tool infer these blocks or not?The single- and dual-port RAMs should be inferred by the synthesis toolautomatically, and for the improved design performance, the adjacent pipelinedregisters should be picked up automatically at the input and output. For improvedspeed and area, it is essential that the synthesis tool can split the large memories intothe multiple BRAMs and even they should be able to have the address and dataaccording to the functional requirements.If we consider the synthesis tool features, then for the SOC design the synthesistools are smart and intelligent enough due to the use of the algorithms to partition andrealize the design. During prototyping, the designer should take care by using thevendor-dependent features of such tools to achieve the desired results. Most of thetime, we observe that such process is automated in the industry by using the synthesisscripts.But practically what care design team should take: Let me share my experiencewhen I was working on one of the SOC projects during the past decade!
1.The first important point I thought by visualizing the architecture that the designis too complex and needs partitioning.  
2.If my SOC functionality is larger than FPGA  logic density what I should do? Ineed to use the multiple FPGAs.  
3.Is it possible that I can achieve the desired speed of SOCs using FPGA?Practically not because the SOC speed is faster as compared to FPGA. 
4.Is it that my RTL can directly map on the FPGA? Answer is no I need to tweakthe RTL and make the changes and make it FPGA resource compliant; forexample, gated clock implementation in the ASIC and FPGA differs.
 
10.3 The Techniques for the Better Optimization
This section discusses the few optimization techniques during RTL design phase forefficient synthesis results.

--- Page 242 ---
10.3.1 Multipliers and Resource Sharing
Most of the time, we may have observed that the multipliers consume large areas. Dueto that, it always effects on the data arrival time if multipliers are used in the registerto register path. For FPGAs, there are dedicated block of DSP and can be used toimplement the larger area multipliers. Example 4 describes the use of 32 × 32-bitmultiplier using the resource sharing technique.
Example 4: VHDL design using resource sharing for multiplier
library ieee;
use ieee.std_logic_1164.all;
use ieee.std_logic_unsigned.all;
use ieee.std_logic_arith.all;
entity resource_sharing  is
port (a_in,b_in,c_in,d_in: in std_logic_vector (31 downto 0);
sel_in: in std_logic;
y_out: out std_logic_vector ( 63 downto 0));
end resource_sharing;
architecture arch_with_sharing of resource_sharing is
signal sig_1, sig_2 : std_logic_vector (31 downto 0);
begin
P1: process (a_in, b_in, c_in, d_in, sel_in)
begin
          if (sel_in=‘1’) then
          sig_1 <= a_in ;
          sig_2 <= b_in;
          else
          sig_1 <= c_in ;
          sig_2<= d_in;
          end if;
end process; 
P2: process ( sig_1, sig_2)
begin
          y_out <= sig_1 * sig_2;
end process;
end arch_with_sharing;
The synthesis result for the 32 × 32-bit multiplier using common resource asmultiplier is shown in Fig. 10.12. Instead of using the two multipliers to perform the

--- Page 243 ---
“a_in * b_in”, “c_in * d_in” the common resource (multiplier) is pushed on the rightside. The chain of multiplexers to select the desired input is used at the input side.This kind of technique is called as resource sharing, and most of the time, we need touse this during the RTL tweaks. This uses four DSP blocks if synthesis is performedfor xc7a35tcsg324-1.
Fig. 10.12Synthesis result for multiplier using resource sharing
10.3.2 Logic Duplication for the Larger Decoding Logic
The larger decoding logic needs to be partitioned during the RTL design phase. Forthat purpose, we can imagine multiple process blocks in parallel. So consider if wewish to have design of 8:256 decoder, then using the logic design techniques we needseventeen 4:16 decoders and hence larger area. This can be avoided by using RTLtweak to improve the area, and this technique is called as logic duplication. Usinglogic duplication, we just need two 4:16 decoders and 256 AND gates and hence hugesaving of area. This may be ruled out for the ASIC synthesis. as it may consume morearea!Example 5 describes the logic duplication functionality for 4:16 decoder. Thesynthesis is shown in Fig. 10.13, and it infers the two 2:4 decoder and 16 AND gates.

--- Page 244 ---
Fig. 10.13Synthesis result for the logic duplication technique
Example 5: VHDL description for the larger decoding logic using logicduplication
library ieee;
use ieee.std_logic_1164.all;
entity logic_duplication is 
port (data_in  : in std_logic_vector( 3 downto 0);
enable_in : in std_logic; 
data_out : out std_logic_vector ( 15 downto 0));
end logic_duplication;
architecture arch_logic_duplication of logic_duplication is
signal y_0_reg, y_1_reg : std_logic_vector ( 3 downto 0);
begin
process(data_in(1 downto 0), enable_in) 
begin
            if (enable_in =‘1’) then

--- Page 245 ---
                 case ( data_in(1 downto 0)) is
                           when ”00” => y_0_reg <= ”0001”;
                           when ”01” => y_0_reg <= ”0010”;
                           when ”10” => y_0_reg <= ”0100”;
                           when ”11” => y_0_reg <= ”1000”;
                    end case;
                 else 
                      y_0_reg <= ”0000”;
                 end if;
 end process; 
  process(data_in(3 downto 2), enable_in) 
   begin
                   if (enable_in =‘1’) then
                       case ( data_in(3 downto 2)) is
                       when ”00” => y_1_reg <= ”0001”;
                      when ”01” => y_1_reg <= ”0010”;
                       when ”10” => y_1_reg <= ”0100”;
                       when ”11” => y_1_reg <= ”1000”;
                       end case;
           else 
                           y_1_reg <= ”0000”;
                       end if;
  end process; 
            data_out(0) <= y_0_reg(0) and y_1_reg(0);
            data_out(1) <= y_0_reg(1) and y_1_reg(0);
            data_out(2) <= y_0_reg(2) and y_1_reg(0);
            data_out(3) <= y_0_reg(3) and y_1_reg(0);
            data_out(4) <= y_0_reg(0) and y_1_reg(1);
            data_out(5) <= y_0_reg(1) and y_1_reg(1);
            data_out(6) <= y_0_reg(2) and y_1_reg(1);
            data_out(7) <= y_0_reg(3) and y_1_reg(1);
            data_out(8) <= y_0_reg(0) and y_1_reg(2);
            data_out(9) <= y_0_reg(1) and y_1_reg(2);         
            data_out(10) <= y_0_reg(2) and y_1_reg(2);
            data_out(11) <= y_0_reg(3) and y_1_reg(2);

--- Page 246 ---
            data_out(12) <= y_0_reg(0) and y_1_reg(3);
            data_out(13) <= y_0_reg(1) and y_1_reg(3);
            data_out(14) <= y_0_reg(2) and y_1_reg(3);
            data_out(15) <= y_0_reg(3) and y_1_reg(3);   
            end arch_logic_duplication;
10.4 Strategies for SOC Prototyping
The strategies to achieve the desired goal of area, speed, and power during prototypephase can play an important role during prototyping phase. What should be thestrategy of SOC protoype team? And what should be thought process is addressed inthis section.
1. Using the initial synthesis and early estimation of logic density try to find thesuitable FPGA.  
2. The benchmark figure should be 60% utilization of the FPGA resources.  
3. If the logic cannot be mapped into single FPGA, then try to estimate the multipleFPGAs required for the prototype.  
4. Try to have plan for the RTL tweaks and synthesis tweaks to get the FPGAequivalent of the design.  
5. Try to have better understanding of the FPGA architecture and functional blocksso that the dedicated required blocks can be used to realize the prototype. 
6. Have better understanding of the IPs, analog and digital blocks and have strategyin place for the analog boards interfacing with the FPGAs. 
7. Have the strategies for the design partitioning at functional, synthesis, and netlistlevel for quick prototype.  
8. Use the block-level, top-level, and chip-level constraints and use the timinganalyzers to achieve them during the design synthesis and implementationphase.
 
9. Have the strategy for multiple clock domain designs, IO mappings, pinmultiplexing, high-speed data transfer.  
10.Have a testing plan in place and use the desired test methodologies to capture theresult.  

--- Page 247 ---
11.In the practical scenario, the design may not work at ASIC speed so use theclock scaling and lower down the prototype speed.  

--- Page 248 ---
10.5 Important Points to Conclude the Chapter
The following are important points to conclude the chapter1.The FPGA is the best candidate for the ASIC or SOC prototype due toprogrammable features, low cost, and shorter implementation cycle. 
2.Prototype team should have strategies in place for single FPGA and multipleFPGA designs.  
3.It is recommended to use 60–70% FPGA resources during the prototype. 
4.Have strategies in place for synthesis tweaks and RTL tweaks to get FPGAequivalent.  
5.Use dedicated resources such as DSP blocks, BRAMs, multipliers, transceivers toimprove the quality and speed of the prototype.  
6.Identify the required partitioning tools, test equipment and have test plan in place. 
7.Have the strategy for the interface of the analog boards with the FPGA. 
8.Use the common resource sharing techniques for large density multipliers. 
9.Use the logic duplication techniques for the larger decoding logic.  
This chapter has given us understanding about the FPGA architecture, FPGAdesign flow, and how to prototype using the FPGA. The next chapter discusses theprototype using single and multiple FPGAs. The chapter is important to understandabout the prototype challenges during single and multiple FPGA prototypes andhow to overcome them?

11. Prototyping Using Single and Multiple
FPGAs
Vaibbhav Taraate1

Vaibbhav Taraate
Email: vaibbhavt@gmail.com
“For the large density SOCs, the design may not fit on single
FPGA fabric and hence it should be prototyped using the multiple
FPGAs.”
Abstract
The chapter discusses the prototyping using the single and multiple FPGAs.
The need for partitioning the design, important challenges during the design
implementation, and the guidelines for the design prototyping is discussed
in this chapter. During prototyping, we need to have the FPGA equivalent
for the ASIC RTL, the clock gating conversions and RTL tweaks are also
discussed in this chapter.
KeywordsFPGA partitioning – Multiplexing – LVDS – SerDes – IO
constraints – Synthesis – IO pad
During this decade the complexity of design has grown and the designs
need to be realized for the required functionality to achieve high speed,
low power and minimum area. Meeting the design and optimization
constraints with the FPGA equivalent functionality realization is the main

--- Page 250 ---
objective of the FPGA prototype. In such scenario, depending on the design
complexity the design needs to be prototyped using the single or multiple
FPGAs. The following few sections describe the scenarios and important
highlights about the prototype using single and multiple FPGAs.
11.1 Choosing the Target FPGA
For the prototype as discussed, the best candidate can be FPGA. FPGA is
field programmable and has adequate resources to realize the large density
designs. One of the main important questions which need to be answered is
how I can choose the target FPGA for the design.
The answer to the question is not as simple as we think! The reason
being it requires good amount of experience to perceive the architecture
functionality using the FPGA equivalent blocks. So during the architecture
design of the SOCs most of the time, team might have discussion about the
same. So with reference to this what we need to consider to select the target
FPGA for the prototype is discussed in this section.
1. Architecture understanding: From the architecture, we can get
information about the required resources to implement the logic,
memory requirements, IP requirements, and initial estimate about the
gate count and interfaces required. By considering all these points, we
can choose the target FPGA.
 
2. Understanding of FPGA architecture and resources:
(1)We need to consider about the number of CLBs the FPGA has as
it gives information about the logic density. The combinational
and sequential logic is implemented using the CLB. Consider the
Artix-7 FPGA has 215 K logic cells, and our goal is to use 40–
70% of the resources to implement the custom logic.
 
(2)As discussed in the previous chapter, we need to know about the
memory requirement for the design. So, we need to consider
about the BRAMs available in the FPGA. During the
implementation, the RAM, ROM, and FIFO can be configured
using BRAMs. If we consider Artix-7 family, then it has 143 Mb
 
 

--- Page 251 ---
BRAM. Even we need to consider about the performance of the
BRAM.
(3)For larger density and complex complexity operations, we need to
estimate about the requirement of logic. Consider the large
density multipliers, floating point arithmetic, or real-time data
processing for the audio and video. In such kind of applications,
the ASIC functionality needs to be pushed by using the DSP
blocks. So, how many DSP slices are available in the FPGA and
what is the performance are other important parameters which
need to be considered. If we consider the Artix-7 FPGA, then it
has 740 DSP slices.
 
(4)Now let us understand about the IO availability. As SOCs need
hundreds of IO pins, we need to consider about the IO availability
while choosing the FPGA. What is the IO voltage level and what
is the speed of IO that need to be considered while choosing the
FPGA. Artix-7 FPGA has 500 IO pins, and the IO voltage level is
1.2–3.3 V.
 
(5)The transceivers and other interfaces also need to be understood.
The Artix-7 FPGA has 16 transceivers and having the speed of
6.6 Gb/s.
 
(6)Apart from all above, we need to think about the other interfaces
such as PCIe and maximum permissible clock speed for the
FPGA. Obviously, it is dependent on the FPGA architecture and
speed of the IO interfaces.
 
11.2 SOC Prototyping Using Single FPGA
Imagine a design scenario to prototype the design shown in Fig. 11.1.

--- Page 252 ---
Fig. 11.1 SOC on single FPGA
From the architecture understanding and from the initial synthesis, the
team can get information about the gate count requirements. For example, if
the logic density requirement is 200,000 gates and the FPGA family has the
same number of gate counts, then it is always wise decision to choose the
FPGA of high gate count family. The following are few of the important
points to choose the FPGA, while prototype for the moderate gate count
designs. The thumb rule during prototype is, design should use of 60–70%
of the FPGA resources during prototype.
The following are few of the important points to choose the FPGA,
during prototype for the moderate gate count designs.
1. Have better understanding of the Architecture: Try to have the
better understanding of the architecture and initial synthesis results to
find the estimation of the FPGA resources required. Consider the small
logic density block of the processor. If only 16–20 instructions to
 

--- Page 253 ---
perform the arithmetic, logical, data transfer, and branching operations
are required, then it can be accommodated in small portion of FPGAs.
If initial gate count estimation is 10,000 gates, then try to find the CLB
count while choosing the FPGA.
2. Internal Memory requirements: Try to find the internal storage
required for the design. If internal memory requirement is 64 Kbyte,
then try to find the available BRAMs from FPGA architecture
datasheets.
 
3. Required DSP functionality: If the DSP functionality such as filters,
multiply, and accumulate is required, then the large density multiplier
chain needs to be pushed using the DSP blocks. In such scenario, try to
identify the complexity of the DSP functionality and then choose the
suitable FPGA which can support the implementation using the DSP
blocks.
 
4. Required FPGA interfaces: During prototype to achieve the target
speed, it is essential to use the high-speed interfaces. So, the prototype
team should identify the different IOs according to voltage levels and
data transfer speed.
 
5. Requirement of high-speed IOs: Depending on the requirement of
speed, try to target the prototype using the high-speed IOs to data
transfer between the FPGA and other boards.
 
6. Partitioning and pin multiplexing: For better prototype, partition the
design across sequential boundaries and use the TDM and LVDS for
the data transfer across the FPGA boundaries.
 
7. Support for the IPs: Try to understand whether the FPGA architecture
can support the configurable or hard IP cores. Depending on the
requirement, choose the FPGA family.
 
8. Configuration management and test: Try to identify whether the
FPGA family has the dedicated blocks for the configuration
management and for the testing.
 

--- Page 254 ---
11.3 How to Reduce the Risk During the
Prototyping?
Reducing risk during the prototype is the important objective, and for that
purpose we need to have better strategies during the selection of FPGA
prototyping platform. We can think about the architecture level strategies to
estimate the logic density and to identify the interfaces and IPs required.
But it is very difficult or impossible during initial phase to accurately
estimate the logic density required for the design, and even it is difficult to
predict the total number of logic cells for the design. In this context, the
better approach is to perform the synthesis at the end of the RTL design
phase and go through the resource utilization summary. The thumb rule is,
the resource utilization for the SOC design should be in the range of 60–
70% of FPGA resources for the single FPGA design. If the utilization is
more than 70% then it is a better choice to partition the design into multiple
FPGAs. That call should be taken by the prototype team.
For the routing, debug logic and for the additional functionality
requirements or specification improvements additional logic may be
required. So while prototyping or choosing the FPGA, do not consume the
100% FPGA resources.
If the resource utilization results in the utilization of more than 100%,
then definitely the SOC design cannot be mapped on the single FPGA and
may need the multiple FPGAs. Although there are architecture and RTL
tweaks to reduce the area, they may not yield into the efficient prototype
due to high logic cell requirement, routing, and high efforts during
synthesis. Instead of that, find the available LUTs in the FPGA and take the
ratio of the (LUTs required for the SOC design/LUTs available in the
FPGA).
This ratio is useful to conclude the number of FPGAs. But instead of
thinking about the use of 100% FPGA resources, it is better to think about
the max of 60% FPGA resources. If I am the team lead, then I will consider
the FPGA resource utilization around 40–50%.
So, the number of FPGAs = (LUTs required for the SOC design)/(LUTs
available in the FPGA * 40%). This can give the better freedom to the
architecture team to cope up with the architecture/design changes and to
add the test and debug logic for the SOC design.

--- Page 255 ---
For example; consider the Virtex7 7VX200T FPGA has LUTs = 
1,221,600 and design needs 2,100,000 LUTs then the number of FPGA
required = (2,100,000)/(1,221,600 * 0.4) = 4.29 so we can think about the
design using the five FPGAs and for the efficient prototype the design
should be well partitioned!
11.4 Prototyping Using Multiple FPGAs
Consider the SOC design which has the processor for the general-purpose
computing, DDR3 memory controller, and video encoder and decoder IP. If
the design need is of 200,000 logic gates, then the design cannot fit on the
single FPGA of Artix-7. In such circumstances, we need to use the design
partitioning to target the design using multiple FPGAs. For most of the
SOCs, we need to target the prototype using the multiple FPGA
architectures. The FPGAs can be connected using ring- or star-type
topology. Figure 11.2 shows logic on the multiple FPGAs using the cable
connectivity.
Fig. 11.2 Multiple FPGAs during SOC prototype
The following are few of the important recommendations useful during
the prototype using multiple FPGAs.
1. Have better understating of the Design: Try to understand the analog
and digital functionality for the design, and partition the design into the
analog and digital design domains. Use the partitioning tools to have
 

--- Page 256 ---
the better results. The automatic partitioning tools can be used to have
the better partitioning of the design across sequential boundaries.
2. Analog functionality and the additional interfaces: As FPGA is good
candidate to realize the digital design, but practically the design has
both analog and digital blocks. So, try to choose the additional daughter
boards interface FPGA  with the ADC and DAC.
 
3. Efficient use of the resources: Try to have strategy while performing
the partitioning to allow the EDA tool to have maximum 70% of the
FPGA resources. This will allow the prototype team to add the BIST
and debug logic during the board bring-up phase.
 
4. Requirement of IOs and pin multiplexing: The speed of IO is an
important factor which decides the overall performance of the
prototype. There are additional multiplexing strategies that need to be
deployed for the multiple FPGA designs.
 
5. Clocking strategies: Depending on the requirement of the star and ring
topology, it is essential to think about the clocking strategies for the
multiple FPGA designs. The clock skew and other board delays need to
be thought during the debug and the test phase.
 
6. IO interfaces: At the SOC architecture level, the decision should be
made about the prototype feature requirement. Always it is a better
choice to consider about the IO speed, IO voltage, bandwidth, clock
and reset network, external interfaces while designing the prototype
using the single or multiple FPGAs!
 
7. IP requirements: IPs are available in the following format, and the
prototype team needs to use the IPs during the design cycle at the
different stages.
1. RTL Source code of IP: Open source code or the license version of
the IP source code is available. The source code using VHDL or
Verilog is available.
 
2. Soft IP: This type of IP cores is sometimes encrypted versions, and
they need to have some processing during the design and reuse. 
 

--- Page 257 ---
3. IPs in the netlist form: They are available in the form of the pre-
synthesized netlist of the SOC components or Synopsys GTECH.  
4. Physical IP: They are also called as hard IPs, and they are pre-laid
out by the foundry.  
5. Encrypted Source Code: The RTL is protected with the encrypted
key and must be decrypted to get the RTL source.  
8. FPGA connectivity: The prototype team need to think about the ring,
star, or mix-type connectivity for the prototype using the multiple
FPGAs. The following are few of the highlights:
a. Ring-type connectivity between FPGAs:
In such type of arrangement, the multiple FPGAs are connected
to form the ring.
In such type of connectivity, it increases the overall path delay.
As the signal is passing through the FPGA, the equivalent
prototype logic can resemble priority logic. This type of the
connectivity has slower speed as compared to other types of
boards.
If we try to visualize the ring-type connections, then at high
level we can think about the pin connection using such type of
inter-FPGA connectivity. The wastage of IOs cannot be limited in
such kind of the connectivity. The FPGAs are at the downside; IOs
will be wasted, and it is additional overhead to the board designer
and board layout team to connect these IOs to high impedance
states.
 
b. Star Connectivity:
This type of inter-FPGA connectivity is faster as compared to
the ring arrangement due to the direct connections with the other
FPGAs. For the better prototype performance, use the high-speed
interconnects between the FPGAs and configure the unused pins as
high impendence state.
 
 

--- Page 258 ---
c. Mixed Connectivity:
During the board design and layout, we may use the mix of the
ring-type connections and star connectivity. Such type of
connectivity can have the moderate performance. The boards
available in the market from vendors have fixed connectivity and
may not be suitable during prototyping as they do not match the
specifications and requirements. Under such circumstances
depending on the design complexity, it is better to choose interface
connectivity for better prototype performance.
 
11.5 Deferred Interconnects
It is very much advisable to use the deferred interconnect using the cables
in the multiple FPGA platforms. As connections are not fixed, it gives
flexibility to the prototype team to use the cables with the required
connections. Figure 11.3 shows a multiple FPGA connectivity using the
cables.
Fig. 11.3 Deferred connection between the FPGAs
As shown in Fig. 11.3, the switched routing matrix can be used to
establish the connectivity between the different FPGA boards. The board
layout and design team need to have better startegy to provide this type of
connectivity for the multi-FPGA designs. The number of layers for the

--- Page 259 ---
complex SOC boards can be of minimum 40–50, and these connections can
give the freedom to the prototype team due to programmable features.
This type of connectivity uses the programmable switches with the
switch matrix for establishing the connections between the FPGAs. So
instead of having the static connections, the board connections can be
programmed and configured. Such type of connectivity can be treated as
dynamic connectivity with reuse.
One of the major advantages of this type of connectivity is to add the
debug and test circuitry as and when required on the field or off the field
due to plug-and-play programmable arrangement.
The care should be taken to fix the pin placements during design
partitioning to program the switches, and for that purpose the portioning
EDA tools can be used.
The speed is the important aspect for any kind of prototype, and as
discussed the speed of prototype is dependent on the interconnect
impedances and stray capacitances. The interconnect delays are function of
the wire length and may be prone to cross talks due to the differed
impedances. During the timing estimations, it is required to consider the
delays between the FPGAs (onboard delays) and logic delays (on-chip
delays). In the simple terminology, we can treat the delays as off-FPGA and
on-FPGA delays. To improve the performance of the prototype, it is
recommended to use the high-speed differential signals between the FPGAs
(Fig. 11.4).

--- Page 260 ---
Fig. 11.4 A multiple FPGA connectivity using switch matrix

consider about delays to find out the overall data transfer between the two
FPGAs.

Clock to q delay of the launch flip-flop
The output buffer delay at launch FPGA pad
The input buffer delay at capture FPGA pad
Setup time of the flip-flop
Onboard delay of the interconnects between the FPGAs.

input of the FPGA#2 (Fig. 11.5).

--- Page 263 ---
To conclude, the prototype engineer needs to take care of the following
important points during SOC prototyping!
1. RTL coding style and the design mapping on the single or multiple
FPGAs  
2. Complexity of buses and interconnects  
3. IP block use and their timing  
4. Overall % utilization for each FPGA device  
5. IO speed and bandwidth to have the data transfer between the FPGAs. 
11.7 Strategies and Guidelines for the Efficient
Prototyping
Better strategies during the project planning and during the RTL design and
synthesis can result in the efficient prototype. This section discusses the
important strategies and guidelines during the design, implementation, and
prototyping phase.
11.7.1 General Guidelines and Project Planning [1]
What I should think about the efficient prototyping platform to validate the
SOC? This is the first fundamental question which needs to be answered!
The answer is the platform should have the efficient architecture and the
better test and debug plan to achieve the desired performance for the SOC!
The following section gives information about the same:
1. Allocate the primary and secondary responsibilities: It is very much
required to allocate the primary and secondary responsibilities to the
team members working for the prototype. It is always advisable to have
the communication between the RTL design team and the SOC
prototype team. This will yield into the better result, and both teams
will be able to understand about the risk during the design and
prototype.
 

--- Page 264 ---
2. Comparative milestones: It is advisable to compare the result of the
SOC prototype with the golden reference. This comparative method is
useful to understand the stage of the prototype. For example, the
functional converge during verification is 97% and at the prototype
level it is almost 80%. If this information is captured, then the
improvements can be made in the existing design/test and debug
environment to achieve the higher coverage at the board level also.
 
3. Version Control: Use the version control for the software and the RTL
modification as this database is useful during the SOC prototype cycle. 
4. List of deliverables and milestones: Create the database of the
deliverables or milestones for the prototype. The following can be
deliverables for the SOC design:
System requirement specifications
Risk and dependability document
C/C++ functional golden reference design
Architecture of the SOC
Micro-architecture for the SOC
Clocking network and distribution
Pin count of the SOC and their functionality
RTL design versions
RTL verification plan and versions
SOC synthesis and timing scripts with the design constraints
Timing, area, and power reports for the individual functional blocks
Top-level area, timing and power constraints, and the reports
Implementation constraints and target FPGA architectures
Multiple FPGA partitioning and board layouts
Test and debug plans
Technology-Independent Design: Has a practice to use the
technology-independent design. To preserve the RTL, use the
compiler directives.
 
11.7.2 IO Planning and Strategies to Minimize Pin Count [1]
11.7.2.1 IO Multiplexing

--- Page 265 ---
To minimize the pin count, the IO pins can be multiplexed. The section
discusses the different IO multiplexing techniques used in the SOC
prototype.
As discussed earlier, the IO multiplexing can be achieved by using the
1. MUX-based IO multiplexing 
2. SerDes-based IO multiplexing 
3. IO multiplexing using the shifter. 
11.7.2.2 MUX-Based IO Multiplexing
In this type of technique, the multiplexer and demultiplexers are used.
Consider the n:1 MUX receiving the IO signals inside FPGA #1. The FPGA
#1 operates on ‘transfer clock.’ The n IOs are multiplexed and to transfer
data they use the ‘transfer clock.’ The FPGA #2 uses ‘receive clock’ to
receive or sample the IO signals, and they are demultiplexed using 1: n
demultiplexer (Fig. 11.8).
Fig. 11.8 MUX-based IO multiplexing
If n = 4, then 4 IOs multiplexed need to transfer the IO signal at the
clock rate of the n * 4. So, the FPGA#1 system clock is ‘clk’ then the

--- Page 266 ---
transfer clock should be n*clk. The transfer and receive clock for the MUX-
based IO multiplexing should be the same that is they should have the same
clock frequency.
11.7.2.3 IO Multiplexing Using SerDes
This is one of the techniques to multiplex IOs, and in this technique the
SerDes and LVDS are used to transfer the IO signal from the launch FPGA
to capture FPGA.
The launch FPGA can transfer the differential signal using the transfer
clock, and this signal is received by capture FPGA (Fig. 11.9).
Fig. 11.9 LVDS and IO SerDes for serial transfer
11.8 IO Planning and Constraints [1]
For the efficient prototype, the IO planning, documentation, and
constraining them are the important tasks. IO planning using Xilinx Vivado
is described below.
Use the IO planning layout shown in Fig. 11.10 [2].

--- Page 267 ---
Fig. 11.10IO planning layout [2]

clicking on IO planning (Fig. 11.11).

Fig. 11.11 IO planning auxiliary view [2]
